# Author's Note

Sacred architecture seeks to express the ineffable through material resources. Its spatial and temporal settings provide a connective between the human and the sacred. How then is such architecture to be evaluated? Is it to be viewed as an art object or a site of religious practices? Is the art meaningful as a symbol of a metaphysical vision or does the language of design evoked by a world of sensory richness and material resources create a meaning of its own? Does awareness of the religious meanings embedded in the architectural composition of the buildings alter the ways of seeing?

The unresolved tensions between sacred and aesthetic tropes fashion the critical discourse for interpreting monuments with religious significance often in a way that either characterises and objectifies it in technical detail or codifies it into a system of signifiers deriving its meanings from a transcendental source. The challenging task in studying sacred architecture is of co-situating perspectives.

In sacred architecture like temples, autonomous fields such as art, religion, architecture intersect. Their meanings lie in the intersections. Interpreting these intersections implies knowledge of multiple domains and the ability to see connections. The appreciation of the autonomy of knowledge fields and the embeddedness of meanings in diverse ways creates a distinct semiotics of form.

The study of a temple is an opportunity for exploring the way multiple perspectives converge to create a state of mind, to evoke a certain emotion and to articulate a belief system and inspire faith in it. The technical choices have specific evocative functions.

There is a larger context to which the temple belongs, from which it derives its meaning. This is the metaphysic of divinity, the philosophical discourse that seeks to understand God and the First Principles of Being. The temple manifests the un-manifest. In its complexity, and its multi-layered meanings, it offers a unique opportunity to co-locate the study of an architectural design with the study of the religious ideas that form its background. Since a temple is primarily a religious edifice, it is important to take into account its religious and sectarian affiliations along with its architectonic, aesthetic and iconographic facets. The book is an effort in this direction.

Studying a temple through this multi-dimensional, yet integrated approach is indeed a daunting task. It has taken me more than a decade to study the Udayeśvara temple in such a holistic manner from primary sources. The religious imagery has been decoded in

This book is the outcome of the Baden-Württemberg Fellowship that I received from the South-Asia Institute, Heidelberg, Germany from May-July 2003. As such, I express deep gratitude to the South Asia Institute, Heidelberg. I am also grateful to the University Grants Commission, India.

# I

# Architecture and Sculpture of the Udayeśvara Temple

## INTRODUCTION

The Udayeśvara temple at Udayapur, situated in the Malwa region of Madhya Pradesh, ranks as one of the most important early medieval temples of North India. It is a Śaiva temple built in the second half of the 11th century CE by Udayāditya Paramāra who was a scion of the illustrious Paramāra dynasty that ruled over Malwa.

Udayāditya ascended the throne in 1070 CE and had to face many challenges in consolidating his empire. The *Udayapur Praśasti* highlights his military might and speaks of him as “...another sun, as it were, destroying, the dense darkness, in the form of his magnanimous foes with the rays issuing from his strong sword and thereby gladdening the hearts of his people by his splendour.”¹

During Udayāditya’s reign, Malwa witnessed political stability and flourished as a leading centre of religious and cultural activities. Udayāditya extended patronage to art and architecture. He founded the city of Udayapur which is located in modern day Vidisha. In this city, he constructed several temples, the most famous being the temple of Udayeśvara. This temple is in the Bhūmija style of architecture.

The *Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra* of Bhoja Paramāra is one of the earliest *Śilpasāstras* that refers to the Bhūmija style of temple. The style, however, appears so well established that one is led to infer that it had earlier antecedents. It seems that the Paramāras of Malwa developed this style to its maturity. This inference is supported by the *Śilpasāstras* as also the large numbers of Bhūmija temples found in Malwa.² Bhoja Paramāra is known to be well versed in architecture. Amongst the various styles of medieval Indian temple architecture, the Bhūmija is distinctive as regards both its origin and style. Etymologically, Bhūmija means that which is produced or born from the earth, which seems to connote regional affiliations. In this literal sense, it may be understood and interpreted as the native style of Malwa, which has the largest concentration of Bhūmija temples. However, it may be pointed out that Bhūmija temples have also been found in Rajasthan and northern Maharashtra. One may perceive another meaning of ‘earthly origin’. Bhūmija or of ‘earthly origin’ may be seen as a terrestrially evolved form as opposed to other forms which were believed to have a divine or supernatural origin. The origin, thus, is secular, unlike others which owe their origin to various gods and demi-gods. In his *Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra*, Bhoja not only compiled this particular style of architecture, but

his dynasty and he seem to have patronized it too. Yet another meaning of Bhūmija may be seen as born from *bhūmis* or storeys. Thus, the word Bhūmija may also connote that the *kūtas* which are arranged vertically one above the other, right from the ground to the *śikhara*, give the appearance of rising directly from the ground upwards.

The distinctive feature of the Bhūmija style is the *śikhara* with four *latās*$^{3}$ or spines adorned with *caitya*-arch motifs on the central vertical bands of the *śikhara*. This style is identifiable by a unique feature, viz. miniature *śikharas* of diminishing heights on the four quadrants between the *latās*. These miniature *śikharas* are called *kūṭastambhas* and are arranged in rows of three to five vertically and five to nine horizontally. At the bottom of the *latās* on the three sides (north, south and west) are three prominent sculpted medallions within a large *caitya* arch known as *śūrsenaka* (pl. 1). The *latā* on the east side terminates in the *śukanāsa*. The temples of this class are invariably *nirandhāra* (i.e. without ambulatory).

### UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

The temple is built of red sandstone. It faces east and consists of a *garbhagṛha* (sanctum), an *antarāla* (vestibule), a *gūḍhamandapa* (covered hall for devotees) with three *mukhamandapas* (porches) and a low flat-roofed hall called *vedī*, separate, but on the same axis (fig. 1, pl. 2). It stood on an extensive, lofty *jagatī* (platform) and was surrounded by eight subsidiary shrines, quite a few now broken (pls. 2, 3). The original entrance to the temple was by a stairway flanked by over life size figures of *Śaiva pratihāras* or door keepers (pl. 4). The entrance now is from the western side (pl. 2). The sanctum of the temple is stellate-cum-circular (pl. 5), is *saptaratha* on plan and *saptabhūmi* in elevation.

The Bhūmija temples are both square and circular in plan. The Bhūmija temple has a unique plan, where the square of the temple is not subdivided like a grid into a number of parts. The square is made to rotate round its centre and made to stop at regular intervals. The points of the rotating square perform a circle and the original square is inscribed within it. Thus, the buttresses between the main wall projections are to be built by *parivartanā* within the circle. The projections on the main walls facing the cardinal points, however, do not result from rotation of the square, but are planned in the usual way, parallel to the square of the *garbhagṛha*.

### THE ARCHITECTURAL MOULDINGS OF THE EXTERIOR OF THE PRĀSĀDA AND THEIR ORNAMENTATION

The *pīṭha* or the base has five mouldings, viz. *bhiṭṭa* 1, *bhiṭṭa* 2, *jādyakumbha*, *karṇaka* and *grāsapaṭṭī* (pl. 7). The first two are basically two courses of the plinth of the substructure. *Bhiṭṭa* 1 is plain, *bhiṭṭa* 2 is ornamented by leaf (*patra*) and garland (*mālā*) design. It is capped by a shoulder (*skandha*) decorated by a *padmadala* or lotus petal design. The *jādyakumbha* is an inverted cyma-recta moulding, a distinctive, lotus-shaped moulding of the *pīṭha*. This is ornamented by a *manibandha* (band of diamond shapes alternating with rosettes) on the side and *ṭhakāra* (*caitya*-window motif) on the top. The *karṇaka* is an arris moulding, decorated by a *gagārapaṭṭī* or a decorative motif comprising of an arrow between two spirals. The fifth is *grāsapaṭṭī*. This is a band of gorgon heads or *grāsa* or *kīrtimukha*.

Above this is the *mandovara*, i.e. the middle portion of the exterior of the temple comprising the walls. This is the part above the base but below the roof. This comprises the *vedibandha* (typical basal wall mouldings) and the wall frieze. The mouldings of the *vedibandha* are the *khura*, *kumbha*, *kalaśa* (pl. 7). The first two are two basic mouldings of

<!-- page 12 src=20260817_162119.jpg -->

the vedibandha. The khura is decorated by a ratnapaṭṭī (band of diamond pattern). Above a karṇikā (knife-edge moulding) rests the kumbha. This is ornamented by niches looking like miniature shrines (khattikā) which house gods and goddesses. It is also decorated by a manibandha in the middle and its shoulder or skandha is decorated by lotus petals or padmadala. Thereafter, hemmed in between two karṇikās is the kalaśa or a torus moulding. This is ornamented by a gagārapaṭṭī. Above this is the jaṅghā or the upper part of the wall. This is decorated by large ornamented niches or khattakas, housing gods and goddesses. In the manḍovara, the buttresses have the appearance of pillars (pl. 6). These buttresses terminate upwards by capital shaped mouldings. They are superimposed by a heavy, ornamental fillet, a deep recess and chādya or roof-shaped moulding (pl. 8).

Thus, an orderly arrangement of pillar-forms and deep recesses form the exterior walls of the temple (pl. 6). These

extend to the śikhara in the same vertical rows but bear the form of kūṭastambhas or miniature śikharas now (pl. 5). The buttresses, thus, seem to rise from the ground itself (Bhūmija), have the appearance of pillars in the janghā portion and continue in the same vertical alignment forming the kūṭastambhas in the śikhara. The kūṭastambhas,

one below the other, give the appearance of long pearl strings suspended from the śikhara, interrupted only by the flat latās with their gavākṣa lace work in the four cardinal directions (pl. 5). The gūḍhamaṇḍapa has a samvarṇā or bell-roof (pl. 9). The gūḍhamaṇḍapa or covered hall for the devotees has three projecting balconies (mukhamaṇḍapa) on the east, north and south sides. The main entrance is from the east, where a flight of steps leads to the east mukhamaṇḍapa (pl. 10).

# THE ŚIKHARA OF THE GARBHAGṚHA

The śikhara of the garbhagṛha is distinctive of the Bhūmija (pls. 1,

2, 5). On the four cardinal directions are the four flat bands or spines (latās) which are decorated throughout with an intricate lace-work of the gavākṣa design. At the base of the east latā, on the front face, there is a large caitya window or śukanāsa

6

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 14 src=20260817_162129.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

containing exquisitely sculpted images of Śiva and other divinities (pl. 11). At the base of each of the other three latās, i.e. on the north,

south and west, there is a prominent sculpted medallion within a large caitya window, viz. śūrasenaka (pl. 1). In between each latā are seven bhūmis of five kūṭastambhas in each sub-quarter (pl. 1). While the ubiquitous motif of the latā is the gavākṣa, the basic motif of the sub-quarters of the śikhara is the kūṭastambha or a miniature pyramidal śikhara placed over a pillaret. There are five rows of these vertically and seven horizontally. The śikhara thus rises, a majestic arrangement of kūṭastambhas, gradually decreasing in size, towards the skandha (shoulder of the spire), the edges of which articulate the star-shaped plan of the temple. The serrated edge is five pointed in each of the sub-quarters (pls. 1, 12). The same theme seems to be echoed in the corrugated rim of the heavy āmalaka. On the northwest face of śikhara of the garbhagṛha is an unusual sculpture of a human figure which seems to ascend the śikhara (pl. 12). The sculpture exhibits an unusual balance. Its role may be to depict the flag bearer who hoists the flag of victory atop the temple śikhara.⁴

Pl. 6. View of the pillar-like buttresses of the garbhagṛha.

### THE ŚUKANĀSA

The great śukanāsa on the east masks part of the latā and the kūṭastambhas of the śikhara (pl. 11). The mighty caitya of the śukanāsa comprises of two parts. In the lower part, the inner trefoil arches sum up a rectangular, pillared shrine (pl. 13) and in the semi-circular wings to the sides are two dancing goddesses (pls. 14, 15). The upper part is a caitya gavākṣa containing dancing Śiva and goddesses (pl. 11). However, such is the rhythm, proportion and balance of the two parts that they strike the eye not as two separate components, but as a precisely sculpted unitary composition. The circular intrados and the swirling, pointed extrados which circumscribe the frame of the śukanāsa integrate into one large caitya-gavākṣa, at the apex of which is a large kīrtimukha. Two makaras or

crocodiles flank the imposing kīrtimukha. Images of the makaras are repeated again as flanking the extrados. In fact, the violent foliage that flanks the archivolt, by its strangely imbricated surface, is

Following pages 8-9:
Pl. 7. View of the pīṭha and vedibandha mouldings.

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

7

<!-- page 15 src=20260817_162139.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 8. View of buttresses and their crowning members (south-east side of the gūḍhamarṇḍapa).

reminiscent of the makara. This reptilian image is echoed in the thick, undulating, serpentine foliage that fills up the space between the extrados and intrados of the upper gavākṣa. Human figures playing musical instruments sit in the spirals formed by the stem. Nāgaphana

(snake-hood) like images are also seen on the sides of the extrados. However, the ornamentation of the śukanāsa is to be seen in its totality and not in individually varying designs. It depicts a remarkable power of formal organization where animals, foliage and human figures are skillfully synthesized into a design of high quality.

One may notice an interesting design component ornamenting the śukanāsa. It comprises male figures with dhammila hairstyle, clutching flared-neck pots and riding atop the backs of the makara figures (pls. 14, 15). Early examples of this design component are seen on the makaras on the toraṇa and the devakoṣṭhas of the 7th century Draupadi Ratha at Mamallapuram. A variant of this design component which depicts riders on

10

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 16 src=20260817_162143.jpg -->

makara figures with outstretched arms is seen adorning the window lintel of the 7th – 8th century Durga Temple at Aihole and the Daśāvatāra cave at Ellora. It is quite probable that this design component may have travelled from India to Southeast Asia as it is depicted on lintels of Pre-Angkorian Cambodian temples. The earliest textual reference to such figures is seen in the Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa in the context of the rendition of decorative motifs such as palm leaf, makara etc.⁵ It states that the form of makara should be made akin to that of a crocodile. Further, there is a cryptic reference to figures atop makaras as well. These are described as on top of makara and as “acyutaḥ”, i.e. stable and not falling. Acyuta is also one of the names of Viṣṇu. This meaning does not construe

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 9. View of the samvarnā roof of the gūdhamaṇḍapa.

here, as a major god would not be accorded the subordinate postion of a rider on a decorative motif. The term, therefore, refers to ‘non-

falling’, ‘firm’, ‘fixed’: a figure who remains stable on the makara. The term “ṛgyavadᵇ” is also used which does not construe and may be emended to “ṛjvaṣat”, connoting a straight and erect posture. This means that such figures were already known by the 6th century CE. It may be mentioned that the earliest representation of male-female figures standing erect on crocodiles can beseen on the railing pillars of the Amaravati stupa (presently in the collection of the British Museum). They are shown holding either stalks of lotus flowers, offering dishes or even a fragmented pot. The next representation of such standing figures is seen in the Ajanta caves, as for instance, on the garbhagṛha doorframe of cave number 5. These figures may have been the prototypes for the evolved figures seen at Udayeśvara.

In the centre of the upper medallion of the śukanāsa (pl. 11) is a multi-armed figure of Naṭeśa Śiva. He is depicted as performing the tāṇḍava⁷ in the ūrdhvajānu karaṇa.⁸ Most of the hands are broken but one of the right hand forms a khaṭakāmukha near the chest. It is possible that one of the broken left hands may have been an arāla above the knee.⁹ While performing the tāṇḍava, Śiva performed several karaṇas and aṅgahāras. Śiva has a simple conical jaṭā-kalāpa or matted locks, which balance well his squarish face-cut and emphatic chin. There are two devīs dancing on either side and fragmentary figures of gaṇas.

The lower part of the śukanāsa is divided into three parts. Just below the upper caitya-gavākṣa, in the centre, is an ornate framed niche housing a syncretic image (pls. 11, 13). In the arches to the sides are, yet again, two dancing devīs (pls. 14, 15). Both exhibit superbly finished forms, supple and sensuously modelled bodies. There is clear expression of bodily structure and movement. These figures are imbued with vigour and vitality and their stance is clearly suggestive of movement. The goddess to the right of

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

the syncretic image imitates the pose of Śiva, i.e. she, too, performs the ūrdhvajānu karaṇa (pl. 14). Of her left hands, one is near the ear,

another hangs down holding the hour-glass drum or ḍamaru. Her right arms are broken, but the main right arm seems to have been a latā hasta, possibly forming an arāla near the knee. She wears a stanapaṭa or breast-band, tied fashionably in the front with a knot. She is adorned with a three-tiered karaṇḍa mukuṭa ornamented with lotus buds. She wears necklaces, bejewelled arm-bands (keyūra), bejewelled girdle, urudāma or thigh ornament and pādakaṭaka on the feet. To the left is an image of dancing Sarasvatī (pl. 15). She stands for knowledge or vidyā. Her vehicle, the swan, can be seen near her right leg. Though one of her legs is broken, it is clear that it was lifted up, and she, too, performed the ūrdhvajānu karaṇa. Her hands are broken but the upper left holds an hour-glass

drum. She wears a mukuṭa, kuṇḍala, necklaces, girdles, pādakaṭaka and pādajālaka on the feet.¹⁰ Her necklace is particularly noteworthy. It imitates long lotus-buds and such a type of a necklace is ubiquitous in the sculptures of North India in the 11th and 12th centuries CE.

King Bhoja Paramāra was a great patron of learning and the arts and hence Sarasvatī, the goddess of learning, was often depicted in the Paramāra sculptures. She was the presiding deity of the Bhojaśālā of Dhara attributed to Bhoja. She is invoked in a number of Paramāra epigraphs. The Sarasvatī of the Udayeśvara śukanāsa (pl. 15), though fragmentary, exhibits precision and command in chiselling and superior workmanship. It is also interesting that while Sarasvatī, being the goddess of music is invariably associated with the viṇā, this is an unusual image where she is also shown as a dancer. The images of dancing Sarasvatī are quite rare. One may draw attention to a beautiful sculpture of a

The Nāṭyaśāstra describes Śiva as the progenitor of the tāṇḍava dance. It says that after destroying the yajña of Dakṣa, he danced at evening time, the aṅgahāras (of tāṇḍava) in accordance with rhythm and drum beats. Seeing her Lord Śiva thus dancing, his consort Pārvatī performed the feminine or the graceful aspect of the dance.¹² The dance of Śiva and Pārvatī was watched by Nandi,

14 | THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Bhadramukha and all the gaṇas. Thus, while the recaka angahāras or the vigorous movements of the tāṇḍava were performed by Śiva, Pārvatī performed the lāsya, the gentle and graceful aspect of the

dance. This description seems to have been quite popular till later times. Bhagvatpāda Śankarācārya describes the twilight time of a hot day when Śiva, blue-throated like a peacock, dances in the company of devī as the peahen, the sound of thunder being the drum played by Hari and the rain being the tears of ecstasy of devotees as spectators.¹³ The Nāṭyaśāstra mentions that varieties of drums and different types of instruments were played during this dance.¹⁴ The scene of the dance of Śiva and Pārvatī seems to have been sculpted in the śukanāsa at Udayeśvara. Abhinavagupta, the most well-known

commentator on the Nātyaśāstra, explains that during the tāṇḍava dance of Śiva, Nandikeśvara and the other gaṇas of Śiva played the mṛdaṅgas or drums.¹⁵ Thus, in most of the Śiva tāṇḍava sculptures,

the gaṇas are shown playing the drums. According to Abhinavagupta, the tāṇḍava is accompanied not only by the drums but also the viṇā. The viṇā was to be played as an accompaniment for the vardhamānaka¹⁶ which was a complicated beat-structure of the music. Since, Sarasvatī is clearly associated with the viṇā, this seems to explain her presence in the performance of the tāṇḍava. However, the artist has been innovative and shown Sarasvatī not playing the viṇā, but dancing.

<!-- page 22 src=20260817_162220.jpg -->

In the lower half of the śukanāsa, in the centre, is a framed niche topped by a miniature daṇḍacchādya laced by a gagārapaṭṭī (pl. 13). To the side of the pillars are decorations of gajavyālas and apsarās. The shrine houses a syncretic image of Hariharārkapitāmaha, i.e. Viṣṇu, Śiva, Sūrya and Brahmā, though the faces of only the first three are shown here. The figure stands erect in sambhaṅga and has three faces and eight hands. The central figure, wearing boots, is that of Sūrya. The kirīṭa mukuṭa that he wears is worn by Viṣṇu too. The figure is bejewelled, wears a short dhofi and the long vanamālā of Viṣṇu. The presence of the four is indicated by the attributes of the right hands which hold the trident, lotus, conch-shell and varadākṣa (boons-giving hand gesture with rosary). To the left may be seen the thick stalk of the lotus which is mutilated and the discus, the rest of the hands being

broken. The attendant figures and forms of animals, the vāhanas (vehicles) of the deities, seen below are fragmentary. One can,

however, discern Dandī and Pīngala, the attendants of Sūrya flanking him, Nandi and a seated, anthropomorphic Garuda with hands folded (though defaced now). To the left is the fragmentary figure of a horse and possibly traces of a goose.

From the 10th to the 12th century, the syncretic images of the trinity, i.e. Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Maheśa along with Sūrya had become very popular in western and central India; they are described in the Śilpasāstra texts of Rajasthan and Gujarat of this period as Hariharahiranyagarbha or Hariharārkapitāmaha. The Aparājitapṛcchā of Gujarat describes the Hariharahiranyagarbha image saying that the four gods should be amalgamated into one and have four faces, eight hands and should stand straight and erect.¹⁷ The front face should be that of Sūrya holding lotuses, the hands of Rudra should be made to his right holding a khaṭvāṅga (skull-topped mace) and trident, Brahmā at the back should hold a rosary (akṣasūtra) and water-pot (kamaṇḍalu), Viṣṇu, on the left, should hold a conch and a discus.

These composite images of Hariharahiranyagarbha follow closely the injunctions laid in the Śilpasāstra texts. It is interesting that the central deity is Sūrya or sometimes Viṣṇu, but not Śiva or Brahmā. These images have been predominantly found in Rajasthan, Gujarat and Central India in the medieval period. Such images do not form the main image in the garbhagṛha. It is noteworthy that these composite images of Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Śiva and Sūrya have a special place in some of the Śaiva temples of the medieval period. According to the Īśānaśivagurudevapaddhati, the composite images of Sūrya, Brahmā,

Visou and Śiva are indicative of the sun, moon and fire of which elements these gods are the adhiṣṭhātri devatās. Such an image greatly assists in the meditation of Sadāśiva.¹⁸ The composition of the śukanāsa, thus, may be related to the elements of Śaiva Siddhānta philosophy. It is seen to reflect the śuddha addhvā of the Śaiva Siddhānta philosophy. We may see the components of Śiva, Śakti, Sadāśiva, Īśvara and Sadvidyā in the śukanāsa.¹⁹ In the upper medallion of the śukanāsa, the components of Śiva and Śakti are seen. Śiva performing the dance of creation is also the creator of the universe or Īśvara, the controller of the destiny of the jīvas. Sadvidyā is made manifest in the form of Sarasvatī and the syncretic image of Hariharārkapitāmaha aids in the meditation of Sadāśiva.

The ornamented śukanāsa juts out from the śikhara and extends upwards of the antarāla

(pl. 17). The antarāla acts as a narrow passage from the gudhamandāpa to the garbhagrha. Thus, the śukanāsa, when seen from the sides, is noticed to be detached from the curvilinear body of the śikhara. The sides of the śukanāsa, i.e. on the north and the south, depict a five-storied celestial mansion (pls. 18, 19). Each storey of this mansion has a miniature pyramidal roof ornamented with the gavākṣa motif, a kūṭachādya and pillarettes. In each storey are housed seated images of Śiva and Śakti or devī, flanked by tall and voluptuous female attendant figures. There are, in all, five images of Śiva and five images of devī. The northern side enshrines four devī and one Śiva image while the southern side enshrines four Śiva and one devī image. The devī images comprise an eclectic group of Śaivite goddesses including Maheśvarī along with two each from the Gaurī and Navadurgā group. All five of them are depicted with a jaṭāmukuṭa.

On the northern side, counting from the top, are devī images enshrined in the first, second, third and fifth storeys (pl. 18). These have been identified as Mahālakṣmī, Māheśvarī, Sarvamaṅgalā and Rambhā respectively. The fourth storey houses a seated image of Śiva. It is fitting for Mahālakṣmī to be placed in the topmost storey as she occupies the first position in the Navadurgā group. The attributes discernable in her right hands are snake, sword, arrow, trident and bowl. The left hands hold the snake, shield, skull-topped mace, bow and varada hasta.²⁰ In the image of Māheśvarī, the bull-mount, Nandi, can be seen on the left below. She holds the snake, sword, arrow, trident

and skull-bowl in her right hands. The attributes discernable in her left hands are the snake, shield, bow and a broken varada hasta. In the image of the Navadurgā Sarvamangalā, the attributes that can be seen include arrows, lotus, abhaya hasta and varadākṣa in the right hands and noose, trident, bell and water-pot in the left hands. She might have held a vajra which is now unfortunately broken.²¹ In the Śiva image, most of the hands are broken, but of the attributes, the skull-topped mace and the skull-bowl are clear. He wears a jaṭāmukuṭa and a munḍamālā (garland of skulls) around his forehead. Nandi is seated to his bottom right. The head of Nandi is turned back to look at Śiva. The last storey depicts Rambhā, a form of Gaurī. She is identified by her elephant mount, placed below on her right. Most of her hands are mutilated but one can discern the vajra in her upper right hand and the shield, noose and skull-bowl in her left hands.²²

Counting from the top, the south side of the celestial mansion has images of seated Śiva in the first, second, fourth and fifth storeys and an image of devī in the third storey (pl. 19). The first storey depicts Śiva. The attributes discernable in the right hands are snake, arrows, trident and varada hasta. The left hands are seen holding the

snake, shield, bow and water-pot. Nandi is seated to the bottom right. Śiva in the second image appears to be holding a snake in the uppermost right and left hands. The other attributes in the right hands are sword, arrow, trident and varada hasta. In the left hands can be seen the skull-topped mace, shield, remnants of a bow and water-pot. Nandi is seated to the bottom left with a large head peeping out. The third image is of Śriyā, a form of

Also, instead of being seated on a preta (pretāsanā), she holds a preta in her top two hands and seems to be almost hurling it. It is possible that it symbolizes the enemy who is to be destroyed. The attributes discernable in her right hands are the abhaṣa hasta, arrows and the iguana. The left hands are mutilated and only the remnants of the stalk of a lotus, a broken bow and the bowl can be seen.²² In the fourth storey, Śiva holds a sword, ḍamaru, vajra and kapālapātra in his right hands while in the left hands can be seen only the shield and karihasta near the ear. A figure of seated Nandi, in profile, is depicted to the right below. The fifth storey depicts a Naṭeśa image with Nandi and the gaṇas looking up to him.

### THE ŚŪRASENAKAS

At the bottom of the north, south, and west latās are the

three śūrasenakas capped by kīrtimukhas (pls. 1, 5). These are similar to the śukanāsa, but have much smaller dimensions. Here, too, the sides of the gavākṣa partly cover the kūṭastambhas at the back. Here, however, unlike the śukanāsa, the śūrasenaka closely adheres to the

curved body of the śikhara and hence there are no sculptures to the sides. The image in the centre of all three śūrasenakas is that of Śiva Naṭeśa.

In the śūrasenaka of the west latā is the image of Śiva dancing in ūrdhvajānu (pl. 20). The discernable attributes in the right hands are the quiver of arrows, a sword and a trident. The attributes that can be discerned in the left hands are the shield, noose, skull-topped mace and the main hand hangs down over the left leg. Two gaṇas, playing the drum and the flute, are seated below Śiva to the left and right. The pyramidal roof of a miniature rectangular shrine abuts the open end of the gavākṣa at the bottom. This enshrines a beautiful image of Śiva carved with precision and command (pl. 21). The three-tiered jaṭāmukuṭa, the modelling of the body, the ornaments, the beatific expression of the face, exhibit a rare finesse. This was a seated six-armed image. This may possibly

be the six-armed Mṛtyuñjaya.²⁴ In the lowest pair of hands, he holds the rosary and water-pot; the other four are unfortunately broken.

The two in the middle, which seem to turn inwards, may have been in the yogamuññā of meditative gesture. This proposition is strengthened by the fact that his face is also in a meditative pose. Furthermore, as desired by the texts, he wears snake ornaments—snake earrings (sarpa kuṇḍala), snake sacred thread (sarpa yajñopavita) and is fair.²⁵ Since this temple was built for victory, it seems fitting that Śiva-Mṛtuñjaya would help overcome death which is the imminent danger on the battlefield. In the same shrine, Śiva is flanked

by two miniature standing attendant figures or Śaiva pratihāras. They have one hand in varada and the other holds a khaṭvāṅga. The parikara

Pl. 23. View of the śūrasenaka of the northern latā.

or the frame of this shrine is decorated by composite fantastic animals (vyāla figures) and elephants (gaja figures). In the even smaller miniature shrines next to this are figures of four-armed Śiva holding the trident and the serpent in the upper right and left hands respectively and varadākṣa and kamaṇḍalu in the lower right and left hands respectively.

The south śūrasenaka has a very similar image of Śiva dancing in the ūrdhvajānu with similar weapons (pl. 22). In the pyramidal shrine below is a figure of seated Śiva, but the arms are too mutilated for identification. The figure of Nandi can be seen seated on the left below. The northern śūrasenaka, too, has Śiva dancing the tāṇḍava in the ūrdhvajānu (pl. 23). The attributes are somewhat different. The upper arms hold the serpent. In the right hands can be discerned the quiver of arrows and a trident. In the left hands can be seen the bell, the bow and possibly a khaṭvāṅga (club with skull at top). The main arm is not above the uplifted knee as in the west and south śūrasenakas, but is poised obliquely across the torso like the trunk of an elephant. This is the gajahasta of the tāṇḍava

# THE MAṆḌOVARA

This portion of the *garbhagṛha* and the *gūḍhamandapa* (pls. 24, 25) comprises buttresses which have the appearance of faceted pillars on which are carved *khattakas* or niches housing deities. The *salilāntara* or recessed part between these buttresses are carved with lotus scrolls and the figures of celestial maidens or *apsarās*. This pattern for the exterior walls of the temple is the same for the *garbhagṛha* as well as the *gūḍhamandapa*.

There are two sets of framed niches sculpted on the buttresses. On the *kumbha* of the *vedibandha* are the *khattikās*, two on each buttress (one on each side). The *khattikās* have enshrined within them the figures primarily of Umā-Maheśvara, but also the *dikpālas* (guardians of the eight directions), other gods, goddesses and Gaṇeśa (pl. 7).

The larger of the niches or *khattakas* are on the *jaṅghā* of each buttress, one on either side. These project on a console and are comprised of various mouldings. The *khattakas* consist of two pillars which contain the image within, as also the triangular pediment of the shrine, the apex of which touches a serrated moulding, above which is a canopy. Above this, on the central facet of either side of the pillar is sculpted a bell with a heavy chain which

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 27. View of buttresses on the south-east side of the *gūḍhamandapa*.

seems to project out from a gorgon face of a kirtimukha. In fact, this bell, suspended from a chain is an ubiquitous mulit, carved all over the walls of the temple. These khattakas house the various forms of Śiva, Brahma, Caneśa, goddesses and the dikpālas (pl. 6).

## THE SCULPTURES ON THE OUTER WALLS OF THE GUDHAMANDAPA

SCULPTURES ON THE SOUTH-EAST SIDE OF THE GUDHAMANDAPA (pl. 28)
In the south-east side of the gūdhamaṇḍapa, the first image from the east (near the east mukhamaṇḍapa) is that of standing mulit-armed Mahābhairava (pl. 26). Most of the arms are broken, but the top left holds a shield, and the top right a damaru, the bottom right is at the thigh and holds, perhaps, the hilt of a sword. He stands in an abhaitya posture, is naked, and wears a long garland of skulls. He wears a uṅgaphaṇī (snake-hood) head-dress. His face, though now defaced, shows distinct traces of a stylized beard. His cdhana, the dog, stands near his left leg. The description of Bhairava in the Vīṇindharmolāya Mahāpurāṇa is as follows: "Thus I will speak of the making of the form of Bhairava. He should be made having a big belly; round, tawny eyes, terrible face with big teeth, two wide nostrils, a garland of skulls and ornaments of snakes on all sides." Since the Paramāras were strong patrons of Saivism, several Bhairava images have been found in Malwa.

Pl. 28. Śūrya enshrined in a khattaka on the south-east side of the gūdhamaṇḍapa.

Between this image and that of Śiva Nateśa near the walls of the south mukhamaṇḍapa (east side) there are three buttresses projecting on the south-east side, each having two images, one on

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

the east face and the other on the south face (pls. 25, 27). On the buttress next to Bhairava, the image in the khattaka on the east side is that of Śūrya who stands straight in sambhaitya posture (pl. 28). He wears the kiriṇa mukata, bejewelled earrings (ratna kuṇḍala), necklaces, garlands, armlets and boots. He has four hands, the upper two hold lotuses, the lower two are broken. He is flanked by his attendants, Daṇḍī and Piṅgala. Daṇḍī is, according to injunctions, shown to the left of Śūrya and carries a daṇḍā (rod) in his hands. Piṅgala is seen to the right and carries a pen and paper in his hands. On the same buttress, facing south is a standing four-armed image of Śiva holding the trident and the snake and with his cdhana below (pl. 29).

It may be noted that the position of the dikpālas, in this temple, is always on the khattakas of the central or the second buttress of the gūdhamaṇḍapa. On the central buttress, facing east proper is a bejewelled, four-armed image of Indra (pl. 30). Indra is one of the most revered gods in the Rg Veda and finds prolific mention there. He is referred to as having the thunderbolt as his attribute since Vedic times. In fact, it is said that one of the best thunderbolts was made for him to hurl at his enemies. He also holds

26 | THE UDAYESVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 32 src=20260817_162318.jpg -->

the arrows or 'iṣu' or 'sāyaka'.³⁰ In the early Purāṇas, his main attribute continues to be the thunderbolt or vajra. His mount is the elephant, who came out during the churning of the ocean and was given to Indra.³¹ In the Rg Veda, Indra is regularly referred to as seated in his chariot drawn by horses.³² However, he is referred to as holding a long goad.³³ The Aparājitapṛcchā says: "with boons-giving hands, with hands bearing the thunderbolt, goad and water-vessel, seated on an elephant, the thousand-eyed Indra stands to the east."³⁴

In this image, he stands in an abhaṅga posture. He holds a thunderbolt (vajra) in his upper right hand and possibly an aṅkuśa (goad), though defaced, in his left hand. The lower right hand is varadākṣa. The lower left is broken but may have held the kamaṇḍalu. His vāhana, the elephant, is to his left below. The Kāmikāgama describes Indra as "mounted on an elephant, pale complexioned, with vajra, he should be placed in the east, (in such a way) should Indra, the thousand-eyed, be worshipped."³⁵

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 29. Śiva enshrined in a khattaka on the south-east side of the gūḍhamaṇḍapa.

The south face of this buttress houses Agni, another of the eight dikpālas (pl. 31). Agni is the guardian of the south-east quarter. In the Atharvaveda, Agni and Sūrya are said to be the 'adhipati' or lords of the eastern direction.³⁶ Further, in the

Atharvaveda, the guardians of the directions are conceived as 'protectors' and 'arrows' or weapons of the directions.³⁷ Agni was accorded a very high status in the Vedic pantheon. In the Rg Veda, Agni is both the means as well as the end. He is both the sacrificial fire as well as an exalted deity.³⁸ He is called a knowledgeable Brahmin.³⁹ As such, it is not surprising that here at Udayeśvara temple, the book is shown in the hand of Agni. It symbolizes both the Vedic sacrifices and Vedic scriptures. Sacrificial rituals and oblations formed an integral part of Śaiva Siddhānta religious practices as well. Out of the three principal activities of yoga, kriyā and caryā in Śaiva Siddhānta, caryā consisted of daily fire rituals along with Śiva mantras. These were performed in accordance with the Śaiva Āgamic texts. Agni is represented here (pl. 31) as a personification of the yajña as is connoted by the religious book held in his upper left hand. The book here is an aberration from the usual attributes of Agni but is ingeniously placed by the artist to indicate a deeper meaning. The book is usually an attribute of Brahmā and it may be noted that according to the Kāraṇāgama, Agni should be made similar to Brahmā.⁴⁰ The figure of Agni stands in abhaṅga and is bejewelled. His hair is arranged in a jaṭāmukuṭa. He wears a necklace and gem-studded earrings. He has upturned moustaches and a stylized beard which covers the neck and reaches almost up to the chest. He holds a śakti (spear) in his upper right and a book in his upper left hand. The lower hands are now broken, but may have been in the boons-giving gesture and holding the water-pot.⁴¹ The Rūpamaṇḍana describes the hands which "are in varada, hand holding śakti with lotus

and water-pot, similar to a heap of fire, seated on a ram is lord Agni.⁴² Remnants of his vāhana, the ram, can be seen beside his right foot.

On the third buttress, there are two syncretic images on both the faces. On the east face is an image of Ardhanārīśvara, i.e. the right half of the body is constituted by Śiva and the left half by his consort Pārvatī (pl. 32).⁴³ The left half of the body is feminine, having a single breast, rounded thighs and the hip thrusts seductively to the side. The upper left hand holds a lotus and the lower is broken. The right half is constituted by a masculine figure. The upper hand holds a trident and the lower is varadākṣa. Near the right leg of Śiva is the bull Nandi, Śiva's mount. To the left, near Pārvatī's leg, is a lion, Pārvatī's mount. This buttress on the south side, houses a four-armed image of Harihara (pl. 33), a syncretic image of Viṣṇu and Śiva. The proper right side constitutes Śiva and his upper hand holds the trident while the lower is in varadākṣa. The proper left constitutes Viṣṇu. His upper hand holds a cakra (discus),

the upper part of which is fashioned as a stylized lotus. The lower hand holds a śaṅkha (conch-shell). Their tiaras differ in their respective halves; Śiva wears the jaṭāmukuṭa and Viṣṇu, the kirīṭa mukuṭa. Their vāhanas are placed below; Nandi with head raised to the bottom right and Garuḍa personified with folded hands to the bottom left. The Devatāmūrtiprakaraṇa, which discusses syncretic images, refers

to the iconography of the Harihara image also. It says that "Harihara should have Viṣṇu and Śiva in one body, the right half of which is Sadāśiva and the left half is Hṛṣikeśa Viṣṇu and should have white and blue forms respectively. The hands should bear rosary in boons-giving (hand), trident, discus and lotus respectively. To the right side, is the bull and to the left side the king of birds, Garuḍa, should be present.'⁴⁴ The image conforms very closely to this description.

Near the south *mukhamandapa* (east side) is a multi-armed image of Śiva Nateśa (pl. 34). Though the legs of Śiva are broken, yet the lifted left knee shows that it was an *ūrdhvatāṇḍava* image. Most of the hands are broken, but some of the attributes which can be discerned are the sword, arrows, shield, the bell, the bow, *gajahasta*, etc. An attendant can be seen playing the drum below.

Another syncretic image of Ardhanariśvara (pls. 35, 36) may be seen in a long, rectangular panel on the superstructure of the south *mukhamandapa* (east face). The stance is *sambhaṅga* and is thus different from the *abhaṅga* stance of the Ardhanariśvara on the *jaṅghā khattaka*. However, the attributes are the same. The right half, which is Śiva, holds the trident in the upper right hand, while the lower right is in *varadākṣa*. The left half, which is Pārvatī, holds a mirror in the upper left hand and a lotus in the lower

Above the Mahābhairava image (near the south face of the east *mukhamandapa*) are two standing Śiva images holding the trident and snake in upper right and left hands respectively. The lower right hand is in *varadākṣa* (broken in one image) and the lower left holds the water-pot (pl. 25).

The projecting buttresses terminate above in a series of sculpted images just below the *saṃvarṇā* (bell roof). Thus, each main niche or

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

khattaka on either side of the buttress has above it another image in a pillared niche (pl. 35) on the tilaka⁴⁵. There are six images on the tilaka of the south-east gūḍhamandapa, two above both facets of each of the three buttresses. The images on the first and the last tilaka are Śakti images. Hemmed in between these two Śakti images are four images of Śiva.

The first image is that of a three-faced and ten armed devī (pl. 37), placed above the khattaka enshrining the image of Sūrya on

the east face. Of the attributes of the right hands, only two are intact. The second hand from the top holds a lotus bud and the fifth hand, in vitarka mudrā, holds a rosary. The five left hands in order are the ghaṇṭā (bell), daṇḍa (rod), pāśa (noose), pustaka (book) and amṛtakalaśa (pot of nectar). The book or manuscript has imitations of writing. To the bottom left is a bird. This seems to be a syncretic image of Gāyatrī, Sāvitrī and Sarasvatī. The three devīs are also called Vedamātās. The manuscript probably denotes the Vedas. Sarasvatī was much venerated by the Paramāra royal family as the goddess of learning. Gāyatrī and Sāvitrī are closely associated with the worship of Sūrya shown below in the niche of the same buttress. This unique syncretic image, however, shows a

combination of knowledge and martial aspects. The *Veda*, *vitarka mudrā*, *akṣasūtra* all are referents of knowledge. The *pāśa* is an important component in Śaiva philosophy. It is the snare or the bondage of ignorance. Weapons such as *daṇḍa*, *ghaṇṭā* and as also the *pāśa* connote the martial aspects. There is a well-known legend in which Gāyatri assumes the form of a hawk (the bird shown below) and goes to fight the demons or *asuras*, to get back the *amṛtakalaśa* or pot of nectar.⁴⁶ Sūrya worship was popular in Malwa. The Mandasaur inscription of the Gupta monarchs Kumāragupta and Bandhuvarman is a standing testimony to this.⁴⁷ It begins with an invocation to the sun and describes the erection of a sun temple in

the city of Daśapura by a guild of silk-cloth weavers. In this context, it is important to highlight the association of Gāyatri, Sāvitri and Sarasvatī, the three *sandhyās*, with Sūrya. The term *sandhyā* means joining or union of any two divisions. In a day, the three *sandhyās* are recognized along with their personifications. The first *sandhyā* or the early morning sun is personified by Gāyatri. The second *sandhyā* or mid-day sun is personified by Sāvitri. The third *sandhyā* or twilight is personified by Sarasvatī. These three *sandhyās* are considered auspicious for the worship of the sun god. Further, it is believed that Gāyatri is of the nature of Agni, Sāvitri is of the nature of Rudra and Sarasvatī is of the nature of Viṣṇu. The *Kāmikāgama* refers to the consecration and worship of Gāyatri, Sāvitri and Sarasvatī.⁴⁸

Above the *khattakas* which house the images of Śiva, Indra, Agni and Ardhanariśvara, from the east to the south respectively, are the niches of the *tilaka*, all of which house the sculptures of seated Śiva (pl. 38). Distinctive features of these images include the *jaṭāmukuṭa* and ten arms. The attributes held in the hands include the snake, hour-glass drum, trident, skull-bowl, bow, skull-topped mace, *abhaya hasta*. Sometimes, the lower hands vary with the *varada hasta* and the water-pot. The sixth niche on the

32

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

tilaka, above the khattaka enshrining Harihara, houses an image of Māheśvarī (pl. 39), as is attested by the presence of Nandi below her right leg. The figure of Nandi is well-sculpted and well-preserved

too. The hands of Māheśvarī are quite mutilated and the attributes that can be discerned are varada hasta on the proper right and kapālapātra and kamandalu on the proper left.

With regard to the khattikās on the vedibandha of the south-east gūḍhamandapa (pl. 27), there are two types of Umā-Maheśvara images that can be seen. The first or standard type, which occurs most commonly (on other sides of the gūḍhamandapa as well), depicts Umā-Maheśvara seated on a pedestal, without their respective mounts (pl. 40). The second type shows Umā-Maheśvara with their respective mounts, the lion and Nandi (pl. 41). Both these types depict Umā seated on the lap of Śiva, her right arm around his shoulder and face towards him. Her right leg is folded, while the left dangles

discernable in the ten-armed devī image (pl. 52) are snake, bowl, arrow, spear and *varada* in the right hands and a broken bow and water-pot in the left hands. Rest of the hands are broken. She is shown

as three-headed and a big bird is seated below her. This is Hamsavatī, who is the chief Sakti who presides over the *ājñā cakra*. She is described in the *Lalitāsahasranāma Stotra* of the *Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa*.$^{60}$ She is *ṣaḍānanā* or one with six faces. The three frontal faces can be seen here in this image. Her very name suggests that ‘she is the one with the swan’. The large bird seated below her is the swan. The *ājñā cakra* is located on the forehead corresponding to the auspicious *tilaka* put there. As such, the image of Hamsavatī in the *tilaka* of the *gūḍhamandapa* is strategically placed.

The two Bhairava images, above Kiranākṣa and Nirṛti, are seated on a corpse while the one above Yama has a dog beside him. The Bhairava image above Kiranākṣa is seated on a corpse (pl. 53). The right hands are totally mutilated which makes it difficult to identify the attributes held in them. The bottom right hand exhibits the boons-giving gesture. It is possible that the upper right hand may have held the snake since the remnants of its body are visible. The attributes discernable in the left hands are the snake, skull-topped mace and skull-bowl. The Bhairava image above Yama (pl. 54) is depicted with a fearsome face on which traces of his fangs and beard are visible. His matted locks are adorned with skulls. The attributes discernable in his right hands are hourglass drum, trident and varada while the left hands hold the skull-topped mace and skull-bowl. The dog can be seen below Bhairava's left leg. The Bhairava image above Nirṛti (pl. 55) depicts him as seated on a corpse. He wears a nāgaphani headdress composed of rearing

snake heads. He has big bulging eyes and a fine moustache and beard. His beard is depicted in an interesting manner with single fluted lines terminating in vigorous curls lacing his chin. The attributes discernable in his right hands are sword, hourglass-drum, arrows, trident and dagger while his left hands are seen holding the shield, club, bow, skull-topped mace and skull-bowl. By the medieval times,

Here, Bhairavācārya is seated on the breast of a corpse adorned with red sandalwood, garlands and ornaments.⁶¹ In Paramāra sculptures, the image of Bhairava is seen both with the dog and the corpse.

The khattikās on the vedībandha house a seated image of Gaṇeśa below Naṭeśa and seated Umā-Maheśvara images of the standard type (Umā-Maheśvara seated on a pedestal) below both the buttresses and the south kapilī. Gaṇeśa (pl. 56) is seated in lalitāsana and is four-armed. The upper right and left hold the axe and the lotus respectively and the lower left holds the modakas (sweet-meat). The lower right may have held the broken tusk as desired by canonical injunctions, but it is indiscernible now.

SCULPTURES ON THE NORTH-WEST SIDE OF THE GŪḌHAMAṆḌAPA (pl. 57)

As stated earlier, the north kapilī too, has a beautiful Tripurāntaka Śiva image (pls. 50, 58). Between the khattakas near the north kapilī image and the north

mukhamandapa (west face), there are two buttresses with four sculptures (pl. 58).

On the buttress next to the north kapilī, there are two sculptures. The one facing west is Varuṇa (pl. 59), the regent of the western quarter.⁶² He stands in abhaṅga and wears a karaṇḍa mukuṭa. He holds the pāśa in his top right hand and the lower is varadākṣa. The left hands are broken. He stands on a crocodile. Varuṇa is an important

Vedic god. He is lord of the waters and upholder of the cosmic order

of the world, i.e. Ṛta.⁶³ He is referred to as the son of Aditi.⁶⁴ The Ṛg Veda interestingly refers to the pāśa of Varuṇa. The raison d'être of the pāśa is that all the pāśas or fetters by which the human being is tied up should be opened or loosened.⁶⁵ The pāśa was therefore associated with Varuṇa since the Vedic period. The Aparājitapṛcchā describes him thus; "With hands as boons-giving, holding the noose, lotus and water vessel, Varuṇa should be made riding a crocodile and in the west".⁶⁶ The other sculpture, facing north, enshrines a standing male deity (pl. 60) with the attributes broken. However, besides the right foot of the deity can be seen the bull mount, with the front legs folded behind and with a bell hanging from its neck. This is Īśāna, the regent of the north-east quarter.

On the second buttress, the khattaka on the west face enshrines an image of Brahmā (pl. 61). He has a long beard and he wears a jaṭāmukuṭa. His upper right hand holds a lotus and the lower is in saṅdamśa mudrā.⁶⁷ Both his left hands are broken. On the northern face of the same buttress is an image of Kārttikeya (pl. 62). Three of his six faces can be seen and he has four arms. His four arms are broken and hence the weapons held cannot be seen. Nevertheless, the traces of the spearhead can still be seen above the upper right hand. His mount, the peacock (though headless now) can be seen to his bottom right.

Next to the image of Kārttikeya, on the khattaka near the north mukhamaṇḍapa (west face), is a dancing Naṭeśa image (pl. 63). The head is broken and so are most of the arms of the image. Some of the attributes that can still be discerned are the snake, shield, skull-topped mace and bell. A gaṇa may be seen below playing the drum. Śiva's body is twisted

40

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 44 src=20260817_162438.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

in a peculiar pose, the hips are twisted in front into a pose which would not be credible even to acrobats (perhaps only to gods!). A similar pose is seen in the south-west side of the *gūḍhamandāpa* (pl. 43). In fact, apart from the Udayeśvara temple, such a pose is seen in another Paramāra image of Śiva from the Ambarnath temple (Maharashtra) of the 11th century CE (pl. 64). The body has the same peculiar twist; torso and hips are both frontal. This may be the *kaṭibhrānta karaṇa*.⁵⁶ This involves the *bhramarī cārī*⁶⁹ or movement of the hip. In the *bhramarī cārī*, a *kuñcita* foot (toes pointing downwards)

is raised and the thigh of that leg which is oblique is twisted around with the turning of the waist also. The whole body turns round through the turning of the sole of the other foot (which is on the ground) and the turning of the spine.⁷⁰

There are four images (pl. 65) on the *tilaka* of the north-west *gūḍhamandāpa*, two each above both the buttresses. Above the *khattakas* of the first buttress enshrining the images of Varuṇa and Īśāna are seated images of Bhairava and Īśāna respectively. Most of the arms of the Bhairava image are mutilated. However, attributes that can be discerned are the hour-glass drum and skull-topped mace in the proper right and left hands respectively. The arms of the seated Īśāna image are mutilated as well and none of the attributes are seen except for *varada hasta* on the proper right. The face has a calm and serene expression. He wears a *jaṭāmukuṭa* and is seated on his mount, the bull. Above the *khattakas* of the second buttress, enshrining the images of Brahmā and Kārttikeya, are seated images of Īśāna and devī respectively. This image of Īśāna bears semblance to the one described previously. The hands are mutilated and the bull mount is seen below. The attributes discernable in the seated devī image are *sarpa* in the upper right and left hands, *kapālapātra*, *triśūla*, *tomara* (javelin), *vajra* in the right hands and *pāśa*, *dhanuṣa*

and water-pot in the left hands. The lower left hand is broken which could have been in abhaya. She is the goddess Bhuvaneśvari.71 The khattikās on the north-west side of the gūḍhamandapa (pl. 58) house seated images of Umā-Maheśvara below the images of Varuṇa and Īśāna. The image of Umā-Maheśvara, below Varuṇa, depicts them as seated on their respective mounts. The one below Īśāna depicts them as seated on a pedestal, similar to those seen on the khattikās of south-east and south-west sides of the gūḍhamandapa. A seated image of Vāyu with his consort Śivā (pl. 66) is seen in a khattikā below the image of Brahmā. Vāyu is one of the eight dikpālas and is the regent of the north-western quarter. The Aparājitaprcchā describes him thus: “With hands having boons-giving (gesture) and flag, bearing a banner and water-pot, seated on a deer, green in colour is the wind (God) the dikpāla.72 The Kāmikāgama describes him as “variegated, flag in hand and seated on a deer.”73 In this image, he is two-armed (dvibhujo), in accordance with his description given in the Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa.74 He is seated in lalitāsana. He has a strange hairstyle which is probably the vyākulamūrdhajo, i.e. agitated hair, as if standing-up, blowing in the wind.75 His left arm is around Śivā, his consort.76 The attribute in his right arm is

SCULPTURES ON THE NORTH-EAST SIDE OF THE GŪḌHAMANḌAPA (pl. 67)

Pl. 63. Śiva Naṭeśa enshrined in a khattaka near the north mukhamaṇḍapa.

Between the north mukhamaṇḍapa and the east mukhamaṇḍapa, there are three buttresses with six sculptures (pl. 68).

The khattaka near the outer wall of the north mukhamaṇḍapa (east side), has an impressive image of Mahiṣāsurmardini (pls. 68, 69). She is the most popular manifestation of Śakti. The Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa, gives a detailed description of the myth connected with her.⁷⁷ She is said to be the personified form of the wrath of all the gods. She was made manifest by the effulgence that the gods had emitted in their anger. The cause of the anger was the buffalo demon Mahiṣāsura who had perpetuated terrible anguish

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

45

<!-- page 49 src=20260817_162509.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

PI. 64. Śiva Natesa, Ambarnath temple, 11th century CE, Maharashtra.

and fear amongst the gods. Thus, the gods endowed her with various weapons, ornaments and the lion as a mount. Equipped with these various weapons, she killed the demon Mahiṣāsura and led the gods to victory. Her victory symbolizes the triumph of good over evil.

The Aparājitapṛcchā describes the goddess thus: “In the right hands are the spear, sword, thunderbolt, discus and arrow. In the left are the bow, shield, noose, bell and goad. Mahiṣa should be shown below with decapitated head at the feet. Know

that the right (foot) should be on Mahiṣa. The left foot should be shown on a lotus pedestal. That demon should be shown with decapitated head and red-coloured clothes (i.e. blood-stained clothes).

The goddess having seized the forelocks by the left hand, pierces the heart by the spear, and the beautiful (foot?) on the belly of the demon. The lion, who is the mount of the goddess, pounces on top of Mahiṣa, the demon.”78

PI. 65. General view of the tilaka of the north-west side of gūḍhamarḍapa.

The Mānasollāsa describes the goddess thus: “With the stance of tribhaṅgī should be shown Mahiṣāsuramardinī. On her right is (shown) a trident, sword, discus, spear and boons-giving gesture. On the left (she) should be painted bearing a drawn bow, noose, goad, arrow and shield or a

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

46

THE UDAYESVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 50 src=20260817_162514.jpg -->

bell or an axe. Below should be shown the demon Mahiṣa with his neck severed; carrying a sword and shield, pierced in the heart by a spear with blood spouting from the severed place and his body reddened (by the flowing blood). Mahiṣāsura is bound by the nāgapāśa (snake-noose) with terrible eyes, drawn brows and terrible glance. Above the navel should be shown a human figure of fierce aspect of which the left foot is placed on the back of the lion of the goddess. And the left foot of Mahiṣāsura should be shown placed above the buffalo.⁷⁹

The Kāśyapa Śilpaśāstra also gives an elaborate description of the goddess. She is described as “serene, with a dusky complexion, having fleshy thighs and breasts. She wears a breast-band fashioned out of snake skin (nāgatvak stanabandha).”⁸⁰ The breast-band can be seen knotted in the

centre of her breasts in this image. The Ajitāgama mentions that she wears a caṇḍātaka⁸¹ or short skirt, the sash of which can also be seen.

In the above mentioned khattaka (pls. 68, 69) near the north mukhamandapa (east side), the goddess is seen with multiple arms. One of the left hands holds a shield but, unfortunately, the majority are broken. As described in the Aparājitapṛcchā, one of her left hands here seizes the forelocks of Mahiṣāsura and the right foot is placed firmly on him. The goddess has tautly modelled breasts, slim waist

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

47

<!-- page 51 src=20260817_162522.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 68. View of buttresses on the north-east side of the gūḍhamandāpa.

and her stance has various flexions. Her matted locks are fashioned in a stylish manner so as to indeed have the semblance of a diadem round the head. She is moon-faced and beautiful. The serenity of her

expression belies the violence of her action, just as her poise and dignity are contrasted with the dramatic flexions of her body. Interestingly enough, following the canonical injunctions, from the severed neck arises the demon Mahiṣāsura to continue his fight with the goddess. He is now shown in human form and not that of a buffalo. A defaced form of the lion can be seen attacking the buffalo near the right foot of the goddess.

The first buttress adjacent to the Mahiṣāsuramardini image has two sculptures. On the north face can be seen a mutilated figure of dancing Śiva (pl. 68). On the east face is a much defaced standing male-female figure, presumably of Hara-Gaurī (pl. 68). However, one can only conjecture as the figures are damaged beyond redemption and only the defaced form of the figures may be discerned. The Ardhanariśvara image on the south-east side, next to the south mukhamandāpa, corresponds to the Śiva-Śakti image of Hara-Gaurī here.

48

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 52 src=20260817_162526.jpg -->

On the second buttress, facing north, stands Vāyu (pl. 70), regent of the north-west direction. He is slightly pot-bellied. He wears a karaṇḍa mukuṭa and is profusely ornamented. He holds his usual attribute, the patākā, in his right hand. Very close observation shows the crinkled folds of the banner in his right hand which open up and get inflated by the wind. The flared portion of the banner is seen billowing above his head on the left. His mount, the deer, is seen to the left below. On the east face of the second buttress stood a single deity, impossible to identify now as the image is completely destroyed (pl. 68). However, the headless, heavy body of the elephant mount can be seen below. It is possible that the upper left hand may have held the bag of treasures, the creased lower part of which can be discerned. It may be said that this image must have been that of Kubera, as he is the only dikpāla that remains. The positions of Kubera and Īśāna seem to have been reversed as Īśāna is the regent of the north-east quarter and Kubera is the regent of the north direction. Even their headgears seem to have been reversed. The purpose may have been

logic of symmetry of arrangement and thus a harmonious design. The dikpālas with elephant mounts, viz. Indra and Kubera have been placed on south-east and north-east, on the second buttress. The

dikpālas with bovine mounts, viz. Yama and Īśāna have been placed corresponding to each other on the buttresses facing south and north proper.

On the third buttress, facing north is Brahmā (pl. 71). He is bearded, bejewelled, four-armed and standing in an abhaṅga posture. His mount, the goose (now headless), is to his left bottom. In his upper right hand he holds the ladle (srucā)⁸² and in the upper left, a book. The lower right is varadākṣa and the lower left holds a kamaṇḍalu.⁸³ The Mānasollāsa has the following description: “The deity (Brahmā) should be shown with four faces, four arms and beautiful eyes. He should have red earrings, with a long beard and yajñopavīla or sacred thread. The deity should have a black antelope skin and wear a white garment. The right hand should be boons-giving and the other should bear a ladle. The left should bear a water-pot and the other a spoon. He should be shown bearing the four Vedas in the front. On the left side should be shown Sāvitrī and Sarasvatī on the right. In front is the vessel for yajña and (he is) surrounded by sages all around. Sometimes

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 69. Mahiṣāsuramardini enshrined in a khattaka near the north mukhamarṇḍapa (east side).

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

49

<!-- page 53 src=20260817_162535.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 70. Vāyu enshrined in a khattaka on the north-east side of the gūḍhamandapa.

he should be depicted as mounted on a swan and sometimes seated on a lotus. So should Brahmā, the creator of the world, be conceived.'⁸⁴ The Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra says: "of stout limbs, having white flowers (or flower), with a white headdress, he (Brahmā) has an upper garment of black antelope skin, a white garment and four faces. In the left hands should be fashioned the staff and the water-pot. He should also have a rosary and be encircled with a girdle of muñja (grass). His right hand should be blessing the universe. If the lord of the universe is fashioned in this way it leads to the well-being of the whole world.'⁸⁵ On the same buttress, facing east and to the right of Brahmā is Sarasvatī (pl. 72). Her mount, the swan, stands below her to the left.

Next to the image of Sarasvatī, near the east mukhamaṇḍapa, the large khattaka (facing north) houses a much mutilated image of a multi-armed Cāmuṇḍā (pls. 68, 73). The legs are completely destroyed and so are the attributes of the hands. However, her emaciated body can still be clearly discerned. She is standing on a corpse.

Of the Śākta or devī cult, two goddesses seemed to have attained prominence, viz. Mahiṣāsuramardinī and Cāmuṇḍā. The latter is said to have emanated from the forehead of the great Mother-

Goddess Ambikā for the purpose of destroying the demons Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa. Since she slayed Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa, she is designated as Cāmuṇḍā.⁸⁶ The Aparājitapṛcchā describes her thus: "Listen to the attributes of Cāmuṇḍā, the goddess who was the slayer of Caṇḍa. She is emaciated, of cruel form, formidable nose and sunken belly. She is tawny, her hair standing erect, dark-limbed, with red eyes, with flashing eyes and teeth and terrible nails and face. She has a trident, sword and shield, bow and arrow, noose, goad, bell, mirror and thunderbolt. She has the rod, axe, conch-shell, discus, mace and club. She is to be with lifted corners of the mouth (i.e. smiling a ghostly sort of a smile), and bearing a skull garland. Cāmuṇḍā is seated on a corpse and has bells hanging down the shanks. She wears a garment of tiger skin, ornaments of serpents, anklets with deep sound and is another (form of) death to the demons. Thus is the image of Cāmuṇḍā.'⁸⁷ The entire description, thus, paints her to be of terrible, cruel and fearsome form, one who awakens great fear and is verily like death incarnate to the enemies of the worshipper! Small wonder, then, that kings and powerful lords were great devotees of this terrible goddess.

50

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 54 src=20260817_162546.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

The Paramāras of Vāgaḍa seem to have depicted Cāmuṇḍā as the deity in the northern quarters. She is depicted in the northern *khattakas* in the Maṇḍaleśvara temple, Panaheda as also the Śiva temple, nos. 1, 2, 3 at Arthuna and shrine no. 9 of Nīlakaṇṭha Mahādeva at Arthuna.⁶⁸ It is interesting that at the Udayeśvara temple, too, the prominent depictions of Cāmuṇḍā may be seen in the northern quarter, for eg., the above mentioned sculpture and another major sculpture depicted on the northern *latā*. In many of

these Paramāra images, whether at Vāgaḍa or Udayapur, Cāmuṇḍā is depicted with a scorpion on the stomach. The idea is that she playfully sports on her body the scorpion, whose poisonous sting is well known. At Vāgaḍa, a ubiquitous attribute of Cāmuṇḍā was the fish, or the bowl with a fish. Perhaps, this may have been so at Udayeśvara temple too, but since most of the hands of the Cāmuṇḍā images are mutilated it is now impossible to tell.

There are six images on the *tilaka* of the north-east *gūḍhamandapa*, two above both facets of the three buttresses (pl. 68). Here too, similar to the sculptural arrangement of the south-east *tilaka*, there are four Śiva images hemmed in between two Śakti images. The images of seated Cāmuṇḍā and Śiva can be seen enshrined in the *tilaka*, above the images of dancing Śiva and Hara-Gaurī respectively. Cāmuṇḍā is depicted as emaciated and seated on a corpse (pl. 74). Her hands are mutilated so the attributes held in them cannot be identified with certainty. The seated Śiva images (pls. 75, 76) of the *tilaka*, above the *khattakas* enshrining the images of Hara-Gaurī, Vāyu, Kubera and Brahmā,

exhibit a similar iconography. The attributes which occur in varying permutations and combinations are the hour-glass drum, sword, trident, abhaya-hasta, shield, skull-topped mace and skull bowl varying with the snake, bow, arrow and varadākṣa.

It may, however, be pointed out that some of the hands in these images are quite mutilated. The devī image (pl. 77) above the khattaka enshrining the image of Sarasvatī (facing east) is quite intriguing as one of the attributes held in her upper right hand is an elephant. All

her other hands are mutilated, but an hour-glass drum and arrows can be discerned in her right hands and a small cup is held in her lower left hand. A water-pot is placed on her right below. She wears a jaṭāmukuṭa adorned with a crescent moon and is, hence, a Śaivite goddess. Her identity is intimately linked to the Andhakāsuravadha episode described in the Varāha Purāṇa where she emerged from the flames issuing out of Rudra's mouth.⁸⁹ She is Yogeśvarī, the devī who aided Śiva in slaying the demon Andhaka. The elephant, held in her upper right hand, connotes her association with Śiva who killed the elephant demon and wore his hide. In this image, she is also seen wearing the elephant hide around her shoulders, the left end of which neatly abuts the cup in her hand where the blood drops of the elephant hide trickle down into it.⁹⁰ It may also be mentioned here that on the north-east side of the gūḍhamandapa, fearsome goddesses beginning with Mahiṣāsuramardinī in the khattaka near the north mukhamandapa, Cāmunḍā in the khattaka near the east mukhamandapa and Yogeśvarī on the tilaka are enshrined.

Adjacent to the image of Cāmunḍā on the tilaka (facing north), below the saṃvarṇā roof and above the Mahiṣāsuramardinī image, is a splendid three-headed and eight-armed image of Hariharārkapitāmaha facing east (pl. 78). It is fairly well preserved and in the early morning, the rays of the sun illuminate it in entirety. The central image is that of Sūrya. The deity stands erect in sambhaṅga and has a finely modelled body; well muscled torso, slim waist and rather long, slim legs. The face is squarish in the Paramāra idiom with the typical roundish but emphatic chin. The eyes are half-closed in a meditative mode, imparting to the face a beatific expression. Sūrya wears a kirīṭa mukuṭa, kuṇḍalas

52

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 56 src=20260817_162557.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

and the usual ornaments. He is seen to wear knee-length boots. Sūrya is flanked by his two attendants, Daṇḍī and Piṅgala, to his left and right respectively. Daṇḍī has a rod in his right hand and Piṅgala holds a book. Both stand in the abhaṅga mudrā. They wear the karaṇḍa mukuṭa, but like Sūrya, they too are slim and long-limbed. Sūrya stands on a chariot of seven splendid horses (saptāśvarathārūḍham), which is driven by his charioteer Aruṇa or dawn.⁹¹ On either side of the three-headed deity may be seen two mālādhara devas (garland-bearing figures). The attributes discernable in this eight-armed image are of four deities are lotus (Sūrya), snake (Śiva), discus (Viṣṇu) and water-pot (Brahmā). Even though it is a syncretic image, it seems that the artist had been predominantly inspired by the glowing image of

Sūrya! The headdress comprises of only the kirīṭa mukuṭa of Sūrya and Viṣṇu, the matted-locks of Śiva and Brahmā are not depicted. The image of Hariharārkapitāmaha has been rendered here to impart a degree of symmetry by matching it with the syncretic image of Ardhanarīśvara on the corresponding side (south-east of the gūḍhamaṇḍapa). Further, this symmetry is visible in the rendition of both the images, as both stand straight and erect in sambhaṅga. It may again be noted that this symmetry is also noticeable in the position of the dikpālas on the second buttresses of the four sides of the gūḍhamaṇḍapa as well as in their arrangement on the basis of similar vāhanas. This harmony is again visible in depicting Śiva-Śakti images of

53

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 78. Hariharārkapitāmaha enshrined in a long rectangular panel on the superstructure of the north mukhamaṇḍapa.

Ardhanarīśvara and Hara-Gaurī on corresponding *khattakas* on the south-east and north-east side. Apotropaic images of Śiva and Śakti as Bhairava and Cāmuṇḍā near the south and north *mukhamaṇḍapas* also exhibit a similar balance in design.

Near this Sūrya image (to its right) are a couple of Īśāna images (pl. 78). Īśāna is that aspect of Śiva, which represents him as guardian of the north-eastern quarter. The *Aparājitapṛcchā* describes him thus: “with boons-giving gesture, (bearing) a trident, snake and citron, seated on a bull should be made Īśāna, of white hue.”⁹² Here, Īśāna is shown wearing a high *jaṭāmukuṭa* and is bejewelled. It may be noted that the *Ajitāgama* describes the various diadems for the *dikpālas*: Indra with a *kirīṭa mukuṭa*, Agni with a flaming one, Īśāna should have a *jaṭāmukuṭa* and all the rest *karaṇḍa mukuṭa*.⁹³ In his four hands, Īśāna bears the trident and the snake in his upper right and left hands, his lower left bears a water-pot and the lower right is in a boons-giving gesture. Of the two images, one stands in an *abhaṅga* posture and has tall and well-modelled proportions. The bull is to the bottom left. The other, in a miniature *devakulikā*, stands straight and has squat proportions. Īśāna is placed here, perhaps, because he is the guardian of the north-east direction.

In the *khattikās* on the north-east *vedībandha* are seen the standard type of Umā-Maheśvara images in which they are seated on a pedestal (pl. 79).

54

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 58 src=20260817_162612.jpg -->

# SCULPTURES ON THE EXTERIOR WALLS OF THE GARBHAGṚHA

SCULPTURES BETWEEN THE SOUTH KAPILĪ AND SOUTH LATĀ (pl. 80)

The sculptures in the khattakas between the south kapilī and south latā are of Śiva, Gaṇeśa and Gaṇeśāṇī (pl. 80). The first buttress enshrines Śiva on both facets. In one of the images, Śiva is depicted with a tiara of matted locks tied by a garland of skulls (kapālamālā). He is four-armed, the upper left holds the snake and the lower right is in varadākṣa. On the adjacent buttress are the images of Gaṇeśa and his consort Gaṇeśāṇī (pls. 80, 81). The pot-bellied Gaṇeśa stands in an abhaṅga posture. The upper two hands hold the broken tusk and the lotus in the right and left respectively. The lower right holds the axe vertically with the blade pointing downwards. The lower left, which is broken, must have held the dish full of sweetmeats (laḍḍus). On the other side of the same buttress is his consort Gaṇeśāṇī. She,

too, is elephant-faced and pot-bellied. Three of her hands are broken, but the lower left still holds the dish of sweet-meats. The south latā enshrines an image of Vīrabhadra (pl. 81).

It may be noted here that the figures of Gaṇeśa and Gaṇeśāṇī are seen mainly on the khattikās of the vedibandha. Their placement

on this singular khattaka is strategic with regard to the Vīrabhadra image on the south latā. According to the Devatāmūrtiprakaraṇa, Gaṇeśa and Vīreśvara (Vīrabhadra) should be made amidst the mātrkās.⁹⁴ It significantly says that “the form of Vīreśvara should be made projected in front amongst the mātrkās.”⁹⁵ This is apparent in the south latā as six goddesses encircle the Vīrabhadra image. Two face the east, two flank the Vīrabhadra image and thus face south and two face the west. These, taken together with Gaṇeśāṇī and Cāmuṇḍā on the two buttresses flanking the latā are the aṣṭamātrkās. On the south-eastern buttress, Gaṇeśāṇī is seen with her consort Gaṇeśa. On the south-western buttress, are two images of Cāmuṇḍā on each facet (pls. 81, 82). She is sculpted on both the faces of the buttress as she does not have a consort.

The image of Vīrabhadra (pl. 81) on the south latā is mutilated; the head and tusks cannot be seen, as also the attributes held in the hands. The kapālamālā, however, is visible.⁹⁶ According to legend, the gods and Prajāpati were attacked by Vīrabhadra⁹⁷, so the prostrate figure of Dakṣa Prajāpati can be seen, as also one of the other gods who is now headless. The large khattaka at the bottom of the south latā, below the image of Vīrabhadra, may have housed some impressive image but is now completely empty (pl. 42).

The khattikās on the vedibandha of the south kapilī and south latā house seated Umā-Maheśvara images of the standard type, depicting Umā-Maheśvara seated on a pedestal.
