SCULPTURES BETWEEN THE SOUTH LATĀ AND WEST LATĀ (pl. 24)

In the khattakas between the south latā and west latā (pls. 24, 83) are the images of Cāmuṇḍā, Śiva, Agni, Nirṛti, Viṣṇu, an animal headed god and an animal-headed goddess. The first buttress enshrines images of Cāmuṇḍā on both facets (pls. 81, 82). The second buttress enshrines the images of a headless and armless deity (south face) and Bhairava (west face). With regard to the identity of the headless and armless deity (pl. 24), close observation reveals the presence of the humped bull, Nandi, to the left below. The head of Nandi is also broken. It can, therefore, be inferred that this deity is Śiva. The image of Bhairava (pl. 84) depicts him with an interesting headdress comprising of skulls adorning his matted-locks. He has bulging eyes, an upturned moustache and beard. The third or central buttress houses the images of the dikpālas, Agni (south-east face) and Nirṛti (south-west face). Here, it may be mentioned that of the five buttresses of the garbhagrha, the central buttress has the dikpālas. This is similar to the pattern of the gūḍhamandapa. The head and hands of Agni (pl. 24) are completely broken and the only way of identifying him is his mount, the ram, to his

bottom left. Nirṛti (pl. 85) is shown naked. He wears a snake headdress, snake necklace and snake earrings (sarpa kuṇḍala). He has a slight paunch. He holds a shield in the upper left hand and the hilt of a sword is clutched in his lower right hand. The lower part of

the scissors is seen held in his upper right hand. According to canonical injunctions of the Kāraṇāgama, the corpse, as a mount, is shown below.⁹⁸ However, Kāmikāgama terms it as the naravāhana.⁹⁹ The fourth buttress enshrines images of Viṣṇu (south face) and Śiva (west face). It is interesting that an image of Viṣṇu occurs here (pl. 86), as otherwise he is relegated only to the lintel panels above the door-frames and on a single khattikā (seated with his consort Lakṣmī) between the north latā and north kapilī. The Viṣṇu image enshrined in the aforementioned khattaka is identified by the attributes of his upper two hands which hold the discus and the remains of a lotus-topped mace. The position of both these attributes have been reversed. The lower two hands are broken. His mount, Garuḍa, can be seen below, on the left. Since the Udayevśara temple has a strong Śaivite sculptural programme, only few Viṣṇu images are seen. The Śiva image (pl. 87) is identifiable by the trident in the upper right hand, the snake in the upper left hand and the mount Nandi to the right below. The fifth buttress enshrines images of an animal

headed god and an animal-headed goddess. The hands of both are broken. The animal headed god faces south proper while the animal headed goddess faces west proper. The animal headed god (pl. 88) is Nandikeśvara, as a fragment of the trident can be seen above his

lower right hand. Further, a very close observation reveals that above the left ear of Nandikeśvara is a stump where the left horn may have been. The theriomorphic form of Nandi can be seen as a headless humped bull to his left, below. With regard to the position of Nandikeśvara, the Matsya Purāṇa mentions that Śiva should be made surrounded on all sides by Indra, Nandi, all the lokpālas, Gaṇeśvaras, dancing Bhṛṅgīrti etc. They should look joyous and should be eulogizing the lord.¹⁰⁰ The animal headed goddess depicted on the

other facet of the same buttress is Nandikeśvarī (pl. 89), the consort of Nandikeśvara. Here, the stumps of both horns are seen above her ears. The headless theriomorphic form may be seen to her right below.

The main khattaka of the west latā houses a headless image of Vīrabhadra (pls. 5, 7, 90). Unfortunately, the attributes held in his hands are not discernable as all the arms are broken. Vīrabhadra is shown strangling a headless figure, probably that of Dakṣa Prajāpati, with his right leg and a fleeing god can be seen on the side of the pillared niche. Surrounding Vīrabhadra are six mātṛkās, and two more being on adjacent buttresses to the left and right. On the left buttress is seen Nandikeśvarī (pl. 89) while on the right is seen Kaumārī (pl. 91). These collectively form the aṣṭamātṛkā group. Here, we see that

60

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 62 src=20260817_162646.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

the bovine-headed Śaivite mother-goddess, Nandikeśvarī, has supplanted the swine-headed mother-goddess Vārāhī, who is basically Vaiṣṇava in character. This appears as a deliberate attempt to give an exclusive Śaivite character.

The khattaka at the bottom of the west latā houses an image of Kārttikeya (pls. 5, 92). The son of Śiva and Pārvatī, he was the commander-in-chief of all the gods against their battle with the Titans. Of the six faces, the three of the front are seen. Following canonical

injunctions, the image had twelve hands, all (except one which holds a shield) are broken now. The facile modelling of the body depicts a youthful form. He wears a karaṇḍa mukuṭa. The girdle with its beautiful carving and pendant lotus-shaped beads can still be seen. His mount, the peacock, may be seen to his bottom right and a male attendant figure to the left. The Aparājitapṛcchā describes him thus: “With six faces, twelve arms, three eyes, riding a peacock, muscular, with a youthful form, with an arrow, lance, sword, goad, staff and rosary, these six are on the left side and on the right are horn, bow, noose, shield, boons-giving and water-pot. This is Svāmi Kārttikeya.”[iii]

The Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra describes him as follows: “Now is being stated the form of Kārttikeya. It should be like the rising sun, having red garments like the fire. His form should be somewhat child-like, attractive, auspicious, pleasant-looking, with a cheerful countenance, splendid, endowed with energy and vitality and beautiful. Ornamented

with a special variegated tiara, adorned with pearls (pearls and gems), he has six faces or one and carries a shining spear. In the town (he is depicted) with twelve arms, in the kheṭaka (small village) with six arms and in the village with two arms. For those who want well being, he should be fashioned like this. In the right hands, (should be shown) the following weapons, viz. spear, arrow, sword, musṛṇṭhī (a type of weapon) and mudgara (club or mace). One arm, the sixth, is said to be extended. In the left hands should be shown the bow, flag, shield and cock. The sixth should be shown as blessing. Thus endowed

with weapons, he is shown as in battle. At other times he should be shown in a playful form. He should be shown with a goat (chāga) and a cock and with a beautiful peacock. Those who want victory over others should establish the image of Skanda in their towns. In the kheṭaka, he should be fashioned with six faces radiant like the fire. He should be endowed with sharp weapons and ornamented with garlands. In the village, he should be made with two arms, imbued with an attractive and glowing appearance. In the right hand should be a spear, in the left, a cock (emendation followed). It should be fashioned with variegated feathers, of large size and fascinating."¹⁰² Quite a few of the Śaivāgamas give the peacock as his mount and not the cock or the goat. The Mrgendragāma

refers to him as “śikhisamśrayah”, the Ajitāgama refers to him as “mayūravāhanam” and the Kāraṇāgama refers to him as “śikhistham”.$^{103}$

The Mānasollāsa has the following description: “The six-faced god (Kumāra) should be shown be shown riding a fine peacock. He should be shown like the rising sun with ornaments appropriate children. In a sthānīya (district capital), the six-faced god should be depicted with twelve arms and four in a kheṭaka. In a village or forest, he should have two arms. He should have six faces or only one face. The right hand bent down should be either in boons-giving or protective gesture. He should have a lance, noose, sword, arrow, spear, one above the other. (He should hold) a banner, bow and shield in his fist, extended forefinger and a cock in the left and protective or boons-giving gesture and the other hand should hold a sword. On the left should be a lance, noose or cock. On the two-armed Kumāra, the lance is placed in the right and the cock on the left. These are the features of Svāmi Kārttika.”$^{104}$ The Kāśyapa Śilpa Śāstram mentions the two, four or six-armed Kārttikeya with his attributes.$^{105}$ One may also draw attention to the beautiful Kārttikeya on the north-west side of the garbhagṛha (pl. 94).

The khattikās between the south latā and west latā house a variety of images (pl. 83). On the khattikās of the first buttress, below the figures of Cāmuṇḍā, are seen images of standing Umā-Maheśvara and seated Umā-Maheśvara with Nandi below. On the khattikās of the second buttress, below the images of a headless deity and Bhairava, are seen seated Umā-Maheśvara images of the standard type. On the khattikās of the third buttress, below Agni and Nirṛti, are images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type and seated

Gaṇeśa-Gaṇeśāṇī respectively. On the khattikās of the fourth buttress, below Viṣṇu and Bhairava, are images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type and seated Brahmā-Brahmāṇi respectively. The khattikās of the fifth buttress, below the images of Nandikeśvara and Nandikeśvarī, house images of seated Brahmā-Brahmāṇi and seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type.

# SCULPTURES BETWEEN THE WEST LATĀ AND NORTH LATĀ (pl. 93)

The west latā has already been described above. The aṣṭamātrkās surround Vīrabhadra. There are five buttresses (housing two images each) in between the west latā and north latā (pls. 7, 93). The first buttress, adjacent to the Vīrabhadra image (on the west latā), houses the sculptures of Kaumārī and Kumāra on either facet. The khattaka facing west enshrines an image of Kaumārī (pl. 91), the consort of Kumāra, She has three faces and four arms which are broken. She stands in abhaṅga, wears a profusion of jewellery and is chiselled with great precision and command. Kumāra (pl. 94) stands in the khattaka facing north proper. His three faces, crowned with an elaborately ornamented karaṇḍa

64

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 66 src=20260817_162703.jpg -->

*mukuṭa*, form a pyramidal structure. The tiara, as described in the *Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra*, is embellished with pearls and gems.$^{106}$ The face of Kumāra is seen to be youthful, beautiful and meditative. Kumāra is shown with a tall, well-built, slim and youthful body. He stands in a graceful, *abhaṅga* stance. He is four-armed, all unfortunately broken. Traces of his mount, the peacock, may be seen to his bottom right. The modelling of the bodies of both Kaumārī and Kumāra shows perfect proportions and a fluid rhythm. The second buttress houses the image of Śiva (west face) and Bhairava (north face). The image of Śiva (pl. 95) can be identified on the basis of the snake and hour-glass drum held in the upper right and left hands respectively. There is an image of seated Nandi on the left below. The image of Bhairava depicts him as standing on a corpse (pl. 93). The third or central buttress enshrines two male deities. The hands and legs of both are broken, as also the attributes. However, according to the iconographic programme of the temple, the central buttress is that of the *dikpālas*. Close observation reveals that the deity on the west face is Varuṇa (pl. 96) and the one of the north face is Vāyu (pl. 97). The snout of the crocodile mount, next to the left foot of Varuṇa, is discernable even though it is mutilated and the body of the deer mount, next to the right foot of Vāyu, can be seen as well. Varuṇa is the regent of the western quarter and Vāyu is the regent of the north-western quarter. The fourth buttress houses images of Śiva wearing a *jaṭāmukuṭa* on both facets. The one on the west face (pl. 7) is identified by the image of Nandi (now headless) and the trident held in the upper right hand. All the arms of the one on the north face (pl. 93) are mutilated but it is identified by the image of Nandi on the right below. The fifth buttress houses an image of a Śaivite devī on the west face (pl. 93). She wears a *jaṭāmukuṭa* and holds a trident in her upper right hand. On the north face of the same buttress is a headless deity with mutilated arms (pl. 93).

The *khattikās* (pl. 7) on the *vedībandha* of the first buttress, below the images of Kaumārī and Kumāra, house seated Umā-Maheśvara images of the standard type. In the *khattikās* of the second buttress, below the *khattakas* enshrining images of Śiva and Bhairava, are seen images of Kubera (pls. 7, 98) and seated Umā-Maheśvara (standard type) respectively. Kubera is traditionally regarded as the lord of all treasures and wealth, as the *yakṣarāja* (lord of all the demi-gods) and the regent of the northern quarter. He is sometimes shown seated on an elephant (*gajāruḍha*) and his mount is a human (*naravāhana*).$^{107}$ The *Aparājitapr̥cchā* says: “Bearing in his hands the mace, treasure, citron and water-pot, seated on an elephant, should be made Dhanada who should be in the north.”$^{108}$ This image (pl. 98) shows him with four hands. The upper two hands hold a mongoose-shaped bag full of treasure. He holds a citron and a cup (presumably wine-cup) in his lower left and right hands respectively. Since the Kuṣāṇa times, Kubera has also been associated with the wine cup. On his two sides are two female attendants, holding fly-whisks. These are actually the two *nidhis* or treasures (personified as females) Padma *nidhi* and Śaṁkha *nidhi*, standing on stylized lotuses and attending to their master.$^{109}$ The *khattikās* of the third buttress (the *khattakas* enshrining images of Varuṇa and Vāyu) enshrine images of seated Umā-Maheśvara with both the lion and Nandi and seated Brahmā-Brahmāṇi respectively. In the image of Brahmā-Brahmāṇi, the central and right heads of Brahmā are visible while the left is defaced (pl. 99). The upper left hand of Brahmā holds a book while his lower left arm is around Brahmāṇi who is seated on his lap. The attributes in his right hands are not discernable. His right foot is placed on his goose mount below. In the *khattikās* of the fourth buttress, below Śiva and Bhairava, are images of seated Brahmā-Brahmāṇi and seated Gaṇeśa-Gaṇeśāni respectively. In the image of Brahmā-Brahmāṇi (pl. 100), the three heads of Brahmā are visible. He is

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE | 65

<!-- page 67 src=20260817_162709.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 93. View of buttresses between the west *latā* and north *latā* of the *garbhagṛha*.

bearded and wears heavy *kuṇḍalas*. He holds a ladle in his upper right hand and a book in his upper left hand. His lower left arm is around Brahmāṇi. The attribute in his lower right hand is not

discernable. Brahmāṇi is seated on his lap. In the Gaṇeśa-Gaṇeśāṇī image (pl. 101), Gaṇeśa is seated in *lalitāsana* and Gaṇeśāṇī is seated on his lap. His upper right hand holds the axe and the upper left holds a lotus. The lower right holds the broken tusk. Gaṇeśāṇī has her right arm around Gaṇeśa. Housed in the *khattikās* of the fifth buttress (below the *khattakas* enshrining images of a devī and an unidentified headless deity) are images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type and seated Sarasvatī respectively. The image of Sarasvatī (pl. 102), is four-armed and though greatly defaced, it is clear that she holds the book and lotus in her upper right and left hands, the *kamaṇḍalu* in her lower left, while the lower right hand may have been in *varadākṣa*.$^{110}$

The northern *latā* deviates from the other two as it houses two female images in the upper and lower *khattakas*. The one in the upper *khattaka* is that of Cāmuṇḍā (pls. 103, 104), which is now headless. Cāmuṇḍā stands on a corpse (now headless), is naked, emaciated and has a sunken belly with a scorpion on it. Cāmuṇḍā is seen wearing a *muṇḍamālā*. Cāmuṇḍā, like Virabhadra on the southern and western *latās*, is also encircled by six *mātṛkās*, the seventh being Cāmuṇḍā herself and the eighth being Māheśvarī (pls. 103, 104). Māheśvarī is located on the buttress towards the north *kapilī* and is identified by the presence of Nandi below. In the *khattaka* below the image of Cāmuṇḍā, a headless and mutilated Mahiṣāsuramardini image (pl. 104) can be seen.

It is interesting to note that the sculptural programme of the Udayeśvara temple reflects the philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta. Śaiva Siddhānta is essentially a dualistic system where the *upāsanā* of both Śiva and Śakti is important. Of the five components of the *śuddha addhvā*, the two main components are Śiva and Śakti. Śakti dominates the left half of the temple because the female principle is that of *vāmā* or that on the left. Therefore, in the Udayeśvara temple, Śakti images occur both on the left side of the *garbhagṛha* (northern and north-eastern directions) and *gūḍhamaṇḍapa* as well as on the *tilaka* of the *gūḍhamaṇḍapa*. On the *garbhagṛha*, Cāmuṇḍā and Mahiṣāsuramardini images are

enshrined in the upper and lower khattakas of the northern latā. Similarly, on the north-east side of the gūdhamaṇḍapa, the image of Mahiṣāsuramardinī is seen on the northern side and the image of Cāmuṇḍā is seen on the eastern side. Further, as regards the gorbhagṛha, all the three latās are encircled by aṣṭamātṛkā figures. In fact, the temple itself is surrounded by eight devakulikās (which are now destroyed) which in all probability may have housed the aṣṭamātṛkās. This is corroborated by the Kāmikāgama which mentions

that in Śiva temples in villages, the image of Gaurī should be established in eight directions.¹¹¹

SCULPTURES BETWEEN THE NORTH LATĀ AND NORTH KAPILĪ (pl. 104)

In between the north latā and north kapilī, there are two buttresses that house four sculptures (pl. 104). The images, enshrined in two khattakas, are much defaced and mutilated beyond recognition. The khattaka on the north face of the first buttress houses an image of a devī with a jaṭāmukuṭa, who, in all probability is Māheśvarī (pls. 103, 104). She holds a kamaṇḍalu in her lower left hand and the mount Nandi is seen beside her left foot. The khattaka on the east face of the buttress houses an image of a four-armed male diety with a dotted-beard and bulging eyes (pl. 104). The upper right hand holds the

pātra. The lower right hand may have held a sword as the hilt of a sword can be discerned just below. A long rod is seen next to the upper left hand and a mutilated vāhana is seen below the left foot. This male deity is Bhairava. The khattaka on the north face of the second buttress houses an unidentified headless and armless male deity (pl. 104). The khattaka on the east face of the same buttress houses a male deity with a jaṭāmukuṭa. The attributes in all the hands are broken so it is difficult to establish the identity of this deity with

certainty. However, a headless image of a bull can be seen beside the right leg of this deity which indicates Śaivite affiliation.

The khattikās on the buttresses between the north latā and north kapilī (pl. 104) house three images of Umā-Maheśvara and one image of Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa. The khattikās on the first buttress house images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type. The khattikās on the second buttress house images of seated Umā-Maheśvara and seated Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa. In the Umā-Maheśvara image, both their respective vāhanas and Bhṛṅgī are depicted (pl. 105). This image corresponds to the description of Umā-Maheśvara images given in the Śilpaśāstras.¹¹² Śiva is seen holding the trident and citron in his right hands, one of the left arms is around Umā while the other left hand holds a snake. One of Umā's arms is on the shoulder of Śiva while the remnant of a mirror is

seen in the other hand. Nandi is seen on the bottom right and the lion mount is seen on the bottom left. A headless, emaciated figure of Bhṛṅgī is rendered in between. In the Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa image (pl. 106), Nārāyaṇa is seated on the proper right in *lalitāsana* with the right leg pendant and left leg folded. He holds a lotus-topped mace, in the upper right hand. The lower left arm is around Lakṣmī and the upper left hand holds the discus. The lower right hand rests on the thigh. Lakṣmī is seated on the lap of Nārāyaṇa to his left. Her right arm encircles Nārāyaṇa’s shoulder. Her left hand holds a lotus. This is the only Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa image depicted in this temple, tucked away in a shadowed *khattikā*.

The figure of Tripurāntaka, as discussed before, is seen on the north *kapilī* (pls. 50, 104). On the large *khattaka* below the north *kapilī* is a three-faced (the fourth, it is assumed, is at the back) image of Brahmā seated on a lotus pedestal (pls. 104, 107). His front face is bearded, but not so the profile

ones. The hands of Brahmā are broken. On his left side is seated his consort Sāvitrī, on his lap. The image is quite mutilated. Sāvitrī’s head and hands are broken. The *Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa* says: “Brahmā should be made seated on a lotus petal and should have four faces. Sāvitrī should be seated on his left lap. One who understands the religion (knows) that she should be imbued with the colour of the sun and have a rosary in her hand.”¹¹³

# THE MUKHAMAṆḌAPA

There are three *mukhamaṇḍapas*, one each on the east, north and south which lead to the *gūḍhamaṇḍapa* (pls. 10, 25, 67). A flight of steps precedes the east *mukhamaṇḍapa* (pl. 10).

Above the *pīṭha* of the temple are the various mouldings of the *mukhamaṇḍapa* (pls. 108, 109) beginning with the *ṭhakārajāla*, i.e. *caitya* arch pattern and diaper work.

Pl. 104. View of buttresses between the north *latā* and north *kapili* of the *garbhagṛha*.

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

71

<!-- page 73 src=20260817_162741.jpg -->

This is followed by an ornate fillet decorated with diamonds with double volute pattern, viz. the *rājasemaka*. Thereafter is a balustrade or *vedikā*. This consists of short pillars (*stambhikās*) of the *ghaṭapallava* class (pot and foliage decoration). In the interspaces of these pillars are the figures of celestial maidens (*apsarās*) and demi-goddesses in a variety of stances. This is followed by the *chajjikā* (roof-like moulding), *mañcikā* (dias-like moulding) with *ṭhakāra* pattern and *ghaṇṭikā* (bell-like) with *jāla* or diaper work. Above this may be seen the *kakṣāsana* or the slanting back of the high seats of the porches (pl. 108). The *kakṣāsana* consists mainly of the *stambhikā* (short posts) and the *daṇḍikā* (rod-like posts). The former is ornamented by the lotus design and scroll work, the latter has the bead and reel design. This is capped on the top and bottom by the *ūrdhvapaṭṭikā* and *adhopaṭṭikā* decorated with *valli* or scroll design.

The roof of the balcony is supported by dwarf pillars of varying diameters (pl. 109). Their shafts rise from the seats. They are ornamented by the *grāsakiñkiṇikā*, *vidyādhara* figures, *ardharatna* and *gagārapaṭṭī*. Their capitals, a series of widening rings are round, whereas the *kumāras* or bracket figures on top project on four sides giving a semblance of squareness. Above the bracket figures and below the *chādya* or eave of the roof are various ornamental mouldings like the *maṇibandha*, *patrāvalli* and *vandanamālikā* (decorative motif resembling festoon of triangular cloth pieces).

The celestial maidens mentioned above are modelled as slim yet with ample curves, tall, graceful and standing on stylized lotuses. They stand mostly in *abhaṅga* stances with slight hip-shot poses. Some stand with the bodies twisted gracefully with flexions

PL 105. Umā-Maheśvara enshrined in a *khattiikā* on the *vedibandha* between the north *latā* and north *kapili*.

or dancing. They are engaged in a variety of actions (pls. 110, 111), viz. holding a pot, holding a lotus or lotus buds, both hands above the head, gazing in the mirror; dancing, putting vermilion in the hair parting, holding a child, probably shooting an arrow; holding a

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

bird (possibly a parrot) though it is indistinct now, and with a camel. The last, is an interesting and unusual image, which is an aberration when compared to the others. It is seen on the *vedikā* of the east *mukhamaṇḍapa* (south side). A female stands with her body bent slightly to the right. Her left leg is on the ground, while the right is placed on an animal below, which is distinctly a camel (pl. 111). She has a long staff which rests against her left shoulder. She has an austere appearance.

Regarding her identity one may refer to the *Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa* which has this following interesting passage: “Virūpākṣa has wide-open eyes, a long staff, bright face, erect hair, yellow moustache, two hands and a fearsome face. His colour is dark with a ruddy hue. He wears dark garments and profuse

72

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 74 src=20260817_162745.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

ornaments. In his hands there are the staff and the reins. His wife, goddess Ninṛti is dark limbed, has a dark face and carries a noose in hand and should be placed on the left. Kāla (time) is (also) Virūpākṣa and mṛtyu (death) is called Ninṛti. O king! Therefore, they have a dark form and dark garments. The staff in his hand and the reins of the camel signify bondage. His mount, the camel, on his left signifies great delusion. I have narrated to you the form of Virūpākṣa who is the unrivalled lord of the demons”.¹¹⁴ She is probably Ninṛti, the wife of Virūpākṣa. The long, majestic staff against her left shoulder

signifies death (death to the enemies, perhaps!) and the camel signifies delusion. This is because the camel carries people across the desert who keep seeing the mirage of greenery and water as they move. Both the hands of Ninṛti are broken, but may have held the noose and the reins of the camel. The reins, which signify pāśa are important in the context of Śaiva Siddhānta.

The doorways of the mukhamaṇḍapa (pl. 112) which lead to the gūḍhamaṇḍapa inside are elaborately decorated, with their doorjambs picturesquely carved with designs of lotus scrolls (patraśākhā), flying or leaping figures (gāndharva śākhā), pillarets (stambha śākhā) decorated with garland design, pot foliage and triangular leaves (ardha ratna), figural representation (rūpa stambha) etc. The doorway ornamentation of the three mukhamaṇḍapa doors is similar, though they do have differences, as for eg., on the peḍya or the lower block of the door jamb. These are invariably carved with the figures of river goddesses and dikpālas as door guardians. The innermost śākhās or jambs of the south mukhamaṇḍapa door are carved with figures of Cāmuṇḍā and Gaṇeśa on either sides. Though most of these figures are either broken or defaced, yet there are some interesting door guardians like Varuṇa, who is seen standing on his crocodile mount holding a pot.

Pl. 108. General view of the north mukhamandapa.

Both Varuna and Nandikeśvara may be seen on the doors of the north and south mukhamandapa. The figure of Vāyu, with wings

behind, is also seen on the doorjamb of the north mukhamandapa. Vāyu is the regent of the north-western quarter.

The lintel of the door of the south mukhamandapa (pl. 113) consists of foliage scrolls and garland bearing figures (mālādhāras) and the lalāṭabimbas (central images of the door lintel). The lalāṭabimbas house figures of Gaṇeśa below and Lakṣmī above. Gaṇeśa has the usual attributes in his four hands, viz. the axe and lotus in the upper right and left hands respectively and the broken tusk and sweets in the lower right and left hands respectively. Lakṣmī, in the niche above, holds the lotuses in her upper two hands and water pot in the lower left. Above this, the door has the panels of the mātrkās or mother goddesses. Hemmed in between is a decorative ornament of pairs of loving pigeons pecking at a lotus. In the center of four pairs of pigeons is a pair of singers. The mātrkā panel

74

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 76 src=20260817_162758.jpg -->

shows nine goddesses, most of them having the lower right hand in *varada* gesture and the left hand holding a water pot. All are seated in the *ardhaparyaṅkāsana* in pedimented niches. The attributes of the upper hands differ. Starting from the proper right is Brahmāṇi, the consort of Brahmā. She holds the ladle and book in her upper right and left hands respectively. The lower right hand is in boons-giving and the left holds the water-pot. Second from right is Māheśvarī, consort of Maheśvara or Śiva. She holds the trident and the snake in her upper right and left hands, while the lower two hands are in boons-giving and hold the water-pot. Thereafter is Kaumārī, consort of Kumāra. Her lower two hands are similar to the mother-goddesses described above. Her upper right hand holds the spear and the upper left a bird, presumably, a cock. Fourth from the right is Vaiṣṇavī. Her upper right and left hand bear the mace and discus. While her lower right hand is in boons-giving, the lower left holds the conch-shell. In the centre, a goddess holds the water-pot in the lower left hand while the lower right is in boons-giving, her upper right hand holds the mace, while the attribute of the upper left is indistinct. This could possibly be Nārasimhī, the consort of Narasimha. She, however, does not have a leonine face. Next (i.e. fourth from right) is Vārāhī, the consort of Varāha, the great boar incarnation of Viṣṇu. While the body is that of a woman, the face is that of a boar. She holds the mace and lotus in her upper right and left hands and her lower hands are in boons-

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

75

<!-- page 77 src=20260817_162807.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 111. Detailed view of the east mukhamandapa.

giving and bear the water-pot. Thereafter (third from right) is Indrāṇī, consort of Indra. Her lower two hands are similar to those of Vārāhī,

but the upper right and left hands hold the thunderbolt (the lower half of which is partly broken) and goad respectively. The sunken belly, skeletal torso, sagging and shrivelled breasts of Cāmunḍā make her form distinct in the niche second from the left. Her upper right hand holds the trident, the lower, a dagger. The lower left hand bears a skull-topped mace which she supports in the crook of her upper left arm. The upper left hand is at the chin in a gesture of wonder or *vismaya*. The goddess to the extreme left is Mahālakṣmī. She bears lotuses in her upper hands, the water-pot in her lower left and the lower right is in boons-giving gesture.

Above the figures of the *mātṛkās* are depicted the male gods and the *navagrahas* or nine planets (pl. 113). In the five projecting pedimented niches are seated the male gods. In between are four recessed niches housing the nine planets. The first projecting pedimented niche, starting from the right proper, houses Śiva. He can be identified because of the trident in his upper right hand. The lower right hand is in the boons-giving gesture and the lower left holds the water-pot. In the next projecting niche is a three-faced, bearded Brahmā. He holds the book and possibly the ladle (now broken) in is his upper left and right hands. The lower hands are similar to Śiva. The central projecting niche houses a syncretic image of Hariharārkapitāmaha. The hands bear the attributes of Śiva, i.e. trident (right hand) snake (left hand); of Sūrya, viz. lotuses in both the hands; of Viṣṇu, viz. conch shell (right hand) discus (left hand) and of Brahmā, viz. boons-giving and water-pot. In the second niche from the left proper is depicted a seated image of Viṣṇu. He bears a stylized mace in his upper right hand. The head of the mace is carved so as to resemble the petals of a lotus. The upper left hand bears a discus. The lower left holds a conch-shell and the lower right is in boons-giving. The first pedimented

76

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 78 src=20260817_162810.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 112. View of the doorway of the south mukhamandapa.

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYESVARA TEMPLE

77

<!-- page 79 src=20260817_162815.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 113. View of the lintels of the doorway of the south mukhamandapa.

niche from the left proper depicts a seated Gaṇeśa. He is depicted with his usual attributes, viz. axe, lotus, sweets and broken tusk. In between these five projecting niches, are four recessed niches bearing

the images of the nine planets in personified forms, viz. Sūrya, Candra, Mangala, Budha, Śani, Bṛhaspati, Śukra, Rāhu and Ketu.¹¹⁵ Each niche has a pair of planets, as for eg., Sūrya and Candra (first recessed niche from proper right). The first recessed niche from left proper has three planets Rāhu, Ketu and possibly Saturn (because of the peculiar way in which the left leg seems to be placed). In Indian astrology, these three are known to be malefic planets and hence are placed together. In any case, since both Rāhu and Ketu possess only half a body, they actually constitute one entity.

The Aparājitapṛcchā describes Rāhu and Ketu thus: “Rāhu should be with half a body, Ketu should have the form of a cupped hand (a curved body). Then again, the text says, “………..Rāhu is in the middle of a tank. Ketu should have the form of a snake’s tail.”¹¹⁶ Rāhu, is, generally conceptualised in Indian astrology to possess only the head and no body. The image of Rāhu being located in the middle of a tank is certainly unusual, but it is seen here (pl. 114) as Rāhu is depicted as a fearsome head above a tank (indicated by the plinth below). The association of Ketu with snakes is also traditional. In fact, Ketu is known to have only a body and no head. Here, however, his face is shown cupped by a snake-hood. The remaining two niches have the remaining four planets, two in each (pl. 113).

### THE INTERIOR OF THE TEMPLE

The gūḍhamandapa has three entrance doors, one from the front or the east mukhamandapa, the other two doors from the north and south mukhamandapas. The main entrance is

78

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 80 src=20260817_162819.jpg -->

B

from the door of the east mukhamandapa. The narrow entrance vestibule has two large niches on the north and south walls and two heavily sculpted entrance pillars (pl. 115). These are identical to the four main pillars of the gūdhamaṇḍapa but lesser in diameter. The ceiling of this vestibule is rectangular and comprises of an ornamentation of three lotuses and foliate scroll.

The gūdhamaṇḍapa (pl. 116) has four pillars and a sculpture of Nandi in the round. These four main pillars are heavily sculpted (pl. 117). These pillars are square almost upto midway, then polygonal and circular, while the bracket capitals on the top are four sided, giving the semblances of squareness again.

These pillars being a combination of the rucaka (square pillar type) and the vṛtta (circular), may be classified as miśraka, i.e. those pillar types which combine forms from square to polygonal to circular. The square base of the pillars have four miniature shrines on four

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 114. Rahu, Ketu and Saturn on the upper lintel of the doorway of the south mukhamandapa.

sides which house deities like Gaṇeśa, Śiva, Brahmā, Devī, Kubera etc., the last being ubiquitous. Kubera is shown seated in an ardhaparyaṅkāsana, the upper hands hold the bag of treasures (nidhi), the lower left hand holds a ratnapātra (pot of jewels) and the lower right hand holds a caṣaka (wine cup). The major square portion of the shaft of the pillar consists of four shrines, two with the images of Śiva and two of Pārvatī. The bases of these shrines have a frieze of mālādhara devas and other flying figures. The sides consist of pilasters with siṁhavyālas and gajavyālas. Above the head of the deity are thick-stemmed, convoluted lotus scrolls forming an arch, at the centre of which is a kirtimukha. From its mouth emanates chains with jingling bells. This is capped by a pyramidal roof at the apex of which is a kalaśa. The polygonal part of the pillar (pl. 117) is ornamented with bells hanging below heavy chains, which themselves emanate from the mouth of kirtimukhas. Above this is the circular section of the pillar which is adorned by flying vidyādharā figures. Their bodies, closely packed and symmetrically arranged in neat, oblique positions convey the sense of movement, forward yet circular. They carry in their hands swords and shields.¹¹⁷ This modelling seems to follow the description of the Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra which says: “Vidyādharas are considered lower than yakṣas in the scale of bodies. They have splendid garlands and clothes and splendid swords and shields in their hands.”¹¹⁸ It is interesting that one of the earliest depictions of vidyādharas with swords goes as far back as the 5th century CE. Cave 17 of Ajanta shows two flying vidyādharas with swords tucked in their girdles.¹¹⁹ The circular section above the vidyādharas is adorned by a design of eight triangular leaves or half diamonds (ardharatna). The capitals, a series of widening rings are round, whereas the bracket capitals on the top are four pronged. The bhāravāhaka or bracket figures are yakṣas. The figures are

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

79

<!-- page 81 src=20260817_162826.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 115. View of the entrance pillars of the gūḍhamandapa.

80

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

2
1

<!-- page 82 src=20260817_162832.jpg -->

shown with heavy bodies and faces, bulging eyes, mouths open and laughing exuberantly with intoxication, pot-bellied and with ornaments. They wear a fan-shaped headdress, brackets show heavy-bodied, four-armed gandharvas with their legs in flying positions. Anthropomorphic figures of nāgas in namaskāramudrā and with snake hoods and tails are also seen.

The Samāṅgaṇasūtradhāra gives an interesting description of inferior celestial beings like vidyādharas, yakṣas, gandharvas, nāgas etc. It seems that the artisan here was well-versed with canonical injunctions. "...Inferior to the daityas should be made the yakṣas who should (appear) exuberant with intoxication. Inferior to the, yakṣas are the gandharvas and inferior to these are the nāgas. Inferior to the nāgas are the rākṣasas who are cruel and deformed. Vidyādharas are considered lower than yakṣas in the scale of bodies. They have splendid garlands and clothes and splendid swords and shields in their hands."¹²⁰

The square portions of the pillars, as mentioned above, have the sculpted images of Śiva and Tapasvini Pārvatī. In the two niche shrines of the pillars which house Śiva, he stands in a slightly abhaṅga stance. He wears a high, conical jaṭāmukuṭa, symmetrically arranged and adorned with a crescent moon. In the upper right and left hands he holds the trident and snake respectively, the lower left holds the water-pot and the lower right must have been boons-giving with rosary. Two niches house the images of Tapasvini Pārvatī (pl. 118). Her stance and jaṭāmukuṭa is similar to that of Śiva.

The goddess Pārvatī, the wife of Śiva, occupies an important position in the Śākta cult. She is the daughter of Himavān, the lord of the mountains and his wife Menā. Śiva, the ascetic, is completely impervious to her great beauty and all womanly charms. Kāmdeva (the god of love) strikes one of his arrows at Śiva, to kindle in him love for Pārvatī. The result is that Śiva opens his third eye whose flames destroy Kāmdeva. Thereafter, Pārvatī resolves to win Śiva's ascetic heart by means of severe penance. She afflicts her body by severe austerities, viz. exposing it to the rain, immersing the body in cold water during winter and observing the pañcāgnitapa during summer. As part of the pañcāgnitapa, the tapasvī should be seated amidst four burning fires with the fifth, i.e. the sun blazing over his/her head. Kālidāsa, the greatest poet and dramatist of ancient India, has described the penance that Pārvatī undertakes in her effort to woo Śiva. She performs the pañcatapa during summer, soaks in the rains during the rainy season, immerses herself in water during the winter, sleeps on a stone slab, lives without shelter and does not eat. In his famous work, the Kumārasambhavam, Kālidāsa has described this penance of Pārvatī.¹²¹

The Aparājitapr̥cchā describes the image of Pārvatī thus: "Pārvatī, born of the (Himālaya) mountain, has a rosary, water pot, Śiva(linga) and the chief of the gaṇas (Gaṇeśa) and has on both the sides agnikuṇḍas or pits of fire."¹²² In the pillar niches of the interior of the gūḍhamandapa, Pārvatī is shown with four hands (pl. 118). The upper right and left hands hold the Śivalinga and Gaṇeśa respectively. The lower hands are invariably broken, but the lower left may have held the kamandalu and the lower right must have been varadākṣa. It seems that there were two agnikuṇḍas, one on each side at the bottom, as one may be seen to the bottom left.

The antarāla or the narrow vestibule between the garbhagṛha and the gūḍhamandapa has two pillars which are similar to the two entrance pillars. The two pillars of the antarāla are placed between pier-like 'wall-pillars' on either side of each (pl. 119). These two pier like 'wall pillars' have a number of figural relief sculptures in niches above and around the Śiva-Pārvatī images. This figural representation includes priests, kings, dancing

<!-- page 83 src=20260817_162840.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 118. Tapasvini Pārvati enshrined in a niche on a pillar of the gūḍhamandapa.

three narrow outlets for water to seep out. It has a simple but elegant scroll border of lotuses alternating with lotus buds and is aesthetic and charming to behold. The other purpose, in the past, could have been to keep the drum. The time-keeper, in fact, was an important person in the temple retinue, and he struck the drum at the required regular intervals.

The ceiling of the *gūḍhamandapa* consists of concentric circles contained within an octagonal frame (pl. 123). The four corners are decorated with *kīrtimukhas*. This is the *nābhichanda* class of ceiling. This is the type of ornate ceiling with deep concentric cusped and coffered courses or bands. The outermost course of the ceiling is the *karnadardarikā*. After intermediary bands follows the *rūpakantha*. This is a recessed band in the ceiling ornamented by figures. This band shows figures of Śiva, seated between pilasters holding the trident and skull-topped mace in his upper right and left hands, the right lower hand is boons-giving and the lower left holds a water-pot. This tier is further enriched by twelve beautifully modelled female figures standing atop *vidyādhara* brackets. The females stand in various seductive poses and stances and are *apsarās*. Unfortunately, the arms of most are broken. The celestial figures

below them are in flying or seated positions. With the exception of one, which is an embracing couple, the rest are all single figures. The figures are quite mutilated. The *rūpakantha* is followed by two courses

of *gajatālu* punctuated in between by *gagārapatta* and other ornamental mouldings. The *gajatālu* means an elephant’s palate and is an ornamental design for the ceiling, the motif here being shaped like a palate. Finally, up to the centre, these are three receding rings of *kola*, which is a miniature cusped segment of the ceiling (decorative motif). The *antarāla* ceiling, too, like that of the entrance vestibule is a *samatala vitāna* or a flat ceiling type, rectangular, ornamented with three lotuses (pl. 124).

## THE GARBHAGRHA

The doorway to the *garbhagṛha*, like that of the *mukhamandapas*, has the guardian deities on the *peḍya* and the ornamentation of the mother goddesses, the various gods and

84

personified planets on the lintel and sub-lintels above (pl. 119). The ceiling, adorned by a large lotus and *kīrtimukhas* at the corners, is square contained within two squares. The *yonipaṭṭa* (base of the Śivalinga) is large, fairly high and plain (pl. 125). The *Rauravāgama* describes three kinds of Śaktis, *yoga*, *bhoga* and *vīraśakti*.$^{124}$ *Yogaśakti* is described as the *pīṭha* of the *liṅga*

which is a part of Umā herself. The different parts of the pīṭha are described in the text.¹²⁵ The Śivaliṅga on top is covered with a brass sheet or cover. This brass cover has a mukha (face) on it, and thus it is

It seeks to represent both Śiva's iconic and aniconic forms together in a single image, the former represented by his face (one or more) and the latter by his phallic symbol. # THE VEDI

In front of the main temple is a flat-roofed, square building known as the vedi (pls. 2, 126). It is well ventilated as the walls consist entirely

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

![img-2.jpeg](img-2.jpeg)

86

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 86 src=20260817_162856.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

of stone grills, three each on the north and south sides and two each on the east and west sides. The stone lattice work consists of square

Pl. 123. View of the ceiling of the gūḍhamaṇḍapa.

perforations (*turyasraka jāla*). The stone grill and the two open entrances, one each on the east and west are ornamented by diaper work, and a band of *ratnapaṭṭī* (diamond shaped decorative design), *vandanamālikā*, *maṇibandha* and also *gagārapaṭṭī* inside and outside. The ceiling consists of a square which is itself framed in two more squares with the *kīrtimukha* decoration (pl. 127). The four pillars of the *vedī* are of the *miśraka* or composite type. They are square to half the height, then polygonal and then circular. The pillars and the square brackets on top are similar in form to the pillars of the temple interior. The ornamentation on the part below does not have the shrines of Śiva and Pārvatī. Instead, the *ghaṭapallava* design is innovatively dealt with. While the overflowing foliage (*pallava*) is certainly shown, the *ghaṭa* is more or less a *ghaṇṭā* or bell design. The circular section above the polygonal one shows a *parṇajāla* or lace work of foliage (pl. 127).

Alexander Cunningham, in his Archaeological Survey of India report (compiled between 1874-77) thinks that the *vedī* was used for the preaching of the *Vedas*: “The *bedi*, or the hall for reading the *beds*, or *vedas*, is a low square building with a projection in the middle of each face. It is 23 feet square inside, with a flat roof supported on four massive pillars, each 8½ feet high and 1 foot 10½ inches square, with wide-spreading brackets. The architrave beams have a section of 2 feet square, with mouldings of 18

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

87

<!-- page 87 src=20260817_162904.jpg -->

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 124. View of the ceiling of the antarāla.

inches above, thus making the total height from floor to ceiling just 10 feet. The two side balconies are open and were no doubt once

used as doorways. The other two balconies are closed with massive trellises, and each contains a large flat slab, raised 3 feet above the ground, as seats for the readers of the vedas. The side walls are closed with the same massive trellises carved out of slabs 8 inches thick, so that the building is therefore very well lighted inside. The ceiling is formed in the usual manner, by overlapping stones, the corners of the square being cut off by slabs meeting in the middle of each side, so as to form a smaller square. This being repeated, the original square of 13 feet side is reduced to a small square of only half size, which is covered by a single slab, ornamented with a lotus flower. The whole building is massive and substantial and is still in excellent preservation after the lapse of eight centuries.¹²⁷

According to tradition, immediately after the completion of the temple, the King ordered the vedī to be built for the performance of a yajña or ritual sacrifice. The function of the vedī was probably that yajñas, rituals etc. may be held there. The smoke would not gather inside as it has profuse ventilation. There are two high stone benches on the north and south side for seating of people, possibly the King and the nobility as is indicated by the elevated seats. It is possible that some music and dance may have also been organized with some of the rituals. It is interesting that Kālidāsa, in the Meghadūta, refers to the dancing girls performing during the evening prayers at the Mahakāla Temple at Ujjain.¹²⁸

four entrances, the principal of which was one facing to the east, all having flight of steps guarded on either side by a figure of a doorkeeper.

Of the eight attendant shrines which surrounded the main temple only six are standing in a ruinous condition and sheltered subsidiary gods."129

Now there are remnants of only a couple of subsidiary shrines (pl. 3). The columns of these small, subsidiary shrines are similar to those of the gūdhamaṇḍapa and quite a few of them are sculpted with the images of Tapasvinī Pārvatī and Śiva. It is possible that these may have housed the aṣṭamātṛkās as mentioned above. The temple was built with its opening to the east, with a flight of steps commencing with a moonstone. Two tall, over life-size male guardian figures flanked this flight of steps (pl. 4). This opening is sealed now. The temple approach is from the west now (pl. 2).

"Muhammadan invaders

mutilated the figure sculptures of the temples. Muhammad Tughluq between AD 1336-38 damaged the attendant temples and erected a

mosque at the back of the temple, using the material from the demolished Persian inscriptions in the mosque record its construction by him."130

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 125. View of the Šivalinga in the garbhagrha.

Thus, in the 14th century CE, the western rear of the temple was converted into a mosque (pl. 2). Two archways were built to the left and right of the garbhagrha. These seemed to have served as symbolic entrances to the mosque. They are not only small in size but there is plenty of open space around, making them quite redundant. In fact, one houses a vibrant Garuḍāsana Viṣnu image which probably dates only a couple of hundred years back. The mosque, too, is no longer in use and now serves as a storehouse for all the fragmentary sculptures in and around the temple. The western gate, in its present form, is said to have been made during the regime of Maharaja Scindia in the 18th century. It is, however, logical that the approach and entry from the east to the west must have been changed during the 14th century when the mosque had been built.

In his report of a Tour (1871-72), J.D. Beglar reports thus:

"The temple is built of red sandstone and stands in the centre of a large paved and raised platform; on all sides of the platform there once ran walls, with seats as in section, but this has long disappeared except in the masjid at the back: surrounding the great temple stood at one time seven minor temples as at Pathari, but somewhat differently arranged. All of them have now disappeared or been converted into, and

The temple at the back, if one existed, as I believe it did, judging from the example at Pathari, does not now exist in its place, but, occupying nearly the whole extent of the projecting piece of platform on this side, stands a Muhammadan masjid consisting of a hall with flat roof, supported by four rows of Hindu Pillars, the last row consisting of dwarf pillars resting on the seat, which runs along the back and side walls; there are twelve pillars in each row, the two extreme ones being dwarf ones, resting on the seats; they are not all alike, but some attempt at a symmetrical arrangement of the dissimilar pillars appears to have been made with partial success; the pillars are fine, massive ones, but not otherwise remarkable; at the centre of the back wall exists the niche of the *melrrab*; a few masons' marks and short lines occur on many of the pillars in this masjid, and conclusively prove, if such evidence were at all necessary, that they are Hindu.

In front of the masjid, and somewhat retired, behind the prolongation of the line of the principal panel, at the back of the tower of the great temple, stand, one on either side, two archways, forming, as it were, the entrances to the masjid. These archways are neither large nor in any way remarkable, and were rather built as matters of form, than as real entrances to the masjid, for, the space between and on either side beyond them being open, access to the masjid is easy throughout its whole front; these arches are

inscribed, and Aurangzeb's name, I believe, occurs in them.

The legend regarding this masjid is that the Emperor Aurangzeb, on his return from the Dakhin, passed this place, and seeing this temple, ordered its demolition and the construction of a masjid with its materials. In obedience to his orders the work of demolition was begun by cutting off or otherwise mutilating the sculptured statues that adorn it, and at the present day most of the statues are so mutilated; but at night, the deity of the temple, Mahadeva, appeared to him in a dream, and ordered him on pain of death to desist from his work of demolition, and as an earnest of his threat he would, he said, cause his listener to fall ill. The next morning Aurangzeb found himself ill, and accordingly, afraid of further irritating the deity of the temple, he ordered the stoppage of the work of destruction; vast quantities of gunpowder that had been stored in the temple to blow it up, were taken out and the temple left unharmed, but a masjid was ordered to be erected at the back of the temple, which had been described above, and a mandate of Aurangzeb directed all those who came to pray in the masjid to enter the temple first and having done reverence there to Mahadeva, to enter the masjid.

Whatever the credit attaching to this legend, certain it is that at the present day the temple is frequented by both Hindus and Muhammadans, and the lingam within equally reverenced by both. The object of worship is a large black stone lingam now enclosed in a shell of highly polished yellow metal said to be gold. I was allowed

to enter the sanctum on taking off my boots, but was not allowed to touch or examine the lingam. No opposition was offered to my entering the temple and examining every part of it except the sanctum, which alone I was requested not to enter with boots on."231

Beglar has made an error as regards his statement that Aurangzeb built the masjid. It was Muhammad Tughlak, in the 14th century, who built the masjid and the inscriptions record his name. Alexander Cunningham had corrected this mistake in his Report of Tours (1874-77):

ARCHITECTURE AND SCULPTURE OF THE UDAYESVARA TEMPLE | 91

Pl. 129. Pisanhāri kā Mandir, Udayapur, Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh.

"The beautiful temple of Udayeswara is the great attraction of Udaypur. It is built entirely of a hard fine-grained red sandstone, which is quarried in the hill close by. The colour is almost the same

as that of a well-burnt bright red brick. The group of buildings consisted originally of the great central temple standing in the midst of a square court-yard with a small temple at each of the four corners and a square bedi, or hall for reading the beds, in the middle of each side.

The north-west corner temple and the western bedi were knocked down in the time of Muhammad Tughlak and a masjid was erected in their place, as recorded in the two inscriptions over the two small doorways to the right and left of the temple, which are dated respectively in 737 and 739 of the Hijira. The gateway on the west is not in the middle, and, as it is built of old materials, I have no doubt that it was built at the same time as the masjid.'¹³²

This temple is one of the few in north India that has survived the onslaught of Islamic invasions and one of the best specimens of central Indian medieval architecture. According to local tradition, in 1325 CE, after the conquest of Udayapur, Sultan Muhammad Tughlaq had given orders to raze the temple. However, the temple was not razed and only a part of the Vedic-hall on the west was converted to a mosque, the mehrab of which can still be seen (pl. 128).

The image contains no text. The OCR result "1" is a hallucination and does not correspond to any content in the source image. Therefore, the correct OCR output is an empty string.

Near the temple is a house called Ghariyālon Kā Makān, i.e. the house of the time-keepers of the temple, who claim to have been there for centuries. Their vocation had been to strike the enormous gong in the temple to keep time. Thus, the various rituals would be performed on time. This temple is a living one, where right up to the 21st century, there are the usual morning and evening pūja and aarti. The priests also perform, on request, special pūjas like Rudrāṣṭaka, Śiva Mahimā and Śiva Abhiṣeka. Religious fairs are held in the vicinity of temple on Śaiva festivals. The main festival of the year is on Śivarātri. All the villagers gather here, as also those of the adjacent villages. On this occasion, an unending stream of devotees lines up in front of the temple from the morning to the evening. The four Mondays of the Śrāvana month, i.e. around July (Sāvan ke Somavār) are also significant for the worship of Śiva.

"Within the modern city are three ancient temples; two of these are small and I content myself with a passing mention of them; they are all kept in fair order

and the smaller ones are white washed; all are used for the purposes of worship at the present day; the two minor ones are similar to the small ones at Mahiyar, being simple towers with porticos in front, supported on pillars; access to them is denied."133

Near the Udayeśvara temple is a small temple, later in date, plain and unostentatious, called Pisanhārī kā Mandir, i.e. the temple constructed by a grinder of flour (pl. 129). It is said that a grinder gradually saved money to get this temple constructed. It is interesting that though on the one hand, royalty could patronize temples, on the other, even a grinder could commission a temple where Brahmins performed rituals. There is a seated Umā-Maheśvara image inside the temple (pl. 130). The finely chiselled hair-styles, face-cuts, proportionately modelled bodies are now unfortunately coated with the grime and soot of the lamps burning in front of the image. Umā is seated to the left, on the lap of Śiva. Their respective mounts, the lion and Nandi, are placed below. Bhṛṅgī dances in between the two mounts. Gaṇeśa and Kārttikeya, their two sons, are shown on the sculptural frame below. Below this image, in a rectangular frame, is a sculptural relief of vīṇādhārī Śiva, surrounded by singing and dancing gaṇas.

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

Pl. 130. Sculpture of Umā-Maheśvara inside the Pisanhārī kā Mandir, Udayapur, Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh.

According to the temple authorities, the flags are changed five times a day and there is very long advance booking. Perhaps the flag-bearer at Udayeśvara hoisted the flag after the fulfillment of the wish of being victorious.

5. "Tālastālākṛtiḥ kāryo makaro makarākṛtiḥ
Upariṣṭāttathaivāsya kāryo ṛgyavadacyutaḥ"
Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa, ed. Kshemaraja Shrikrishnadasa (Bombay: Srivenkatesvara Steam Press, 1911), III.54.7. The word "ṛgyavad" has been emended by me to "ṛjivāyat".

6. The variant reading given for ṛgyavadacyutaḥ is ṛkṣevadacyutaḥ in the Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa, 341 (the above edition). The meanings of ṛkṣa are 'bear', 'mountain', 'star'. The term 'bear' does not construe. However, mountain can connote 'firm as a mountain'. One may note that a mountain is called acala. Similarly, the star may also connote the dhruva or the northpole star which appears to be a 'fixed' star.

7. Nāṭyaśāstra of Bharata along with the commentary Abhinavabhārati, ed. Madhusudan Shastri (Varanasi: Banaras Hindu University, 1971). Tāṇḍava is the dance composed and performed by Lord Śiva himself [chapter 4 of the Nāṭyaśāstra]. He taught it to his protégé Taṇḍu [Nāṭyaśāstra, 4.264-65; 4.272]. Thereafter, Śiva asked Taṇḍu to teach it to Bharata muni, the author of the Nāṭyaśāstra [Nāṭyaśāstra, 4.13-18]. All the references to the Nāṭyaśāstra are of the B.H.U edition, unless specified otherwise.

8. Karaṇa is a sequence of hand and foot movements of dance culminating in a pose or stance. The Nāṭyaśāstra [chapter 4] describes 108 such karaṇas, which are depicted in the Chidambaram Temple in Tamil Nadu. A sequence of karaṇas is termed aṅgahāra [Nāṭyaśāstra, 4.29]. The 25th karaṇa is the iirdhvajānu karaṇa [Nāṭyaśāstra, 4.85]. In this karaṇa, a kuñcita foot (i.e. toe pointing downwards) in the same position is raised high up.

9. Abhinavabhārati on Nāṭyaśāstra, 4.85. The hand movement has not been specified in the Nāṭyaśāstra. However, his commentator, Abhinavagupta says that one hand forms the arāla, while the other is a khaṭakāmukha near the chest; Anupa Pande, A Historical and Cultural Study of the Nāṭyaśāstra of Bharata (Jodhpur: Kusumanjali Prakashan, 1991), 144-45. Khaṭakāmukha: the hand first forms a fist, after which the tip of the fore-finger touches the tip of the thumb. Thereafter, the ring-finger and little finger are thrown upwards, separated and slightly bent [Nāṭyaśāstra, 9.61-64]. Arāla: Fingers are separated from each other and slightly bent, the forefinger is curved down like a bow and the thumb bent. [Nāṭyaśāstra, 9.46-52].

10. The pāḍakaṭaka or pāḍavalaya are simple gold rings worn a little above the ankles. The pāḍajālaka has a filigree like design and is worn round the ankles.

The caption there presumes that she is holding a viṇā thought it cannot be seen now. An aṅkuśa can be clearly seen in her upper right hand which is not normally the attribute of Sarasvati.

Vardhamānaka was a complicated and elaborate melody cum beat structure of Gāndharva, which was the ancient Indian classical music. The tāṇḍava was, thus, performed to the accompaniment of the vardhamānaka. This involved the playing of complicated beat structures on the drums, playing on the viṇā and the singing of the śuṣka-gita (melody without lyrics).
