## II

## The Metaphysics of Śaivism and Śaiva Siddhānta

The ancient Indian view about nature, society and art is quite different from the modern view. Tradition sees an inner parallelism between the three. The social order, in its essence, proceeds from gods, just as the order of nature. A common term ṛta signified both of these. Art was also regarded as self-reproduction, a creation by man replicating the creation of the divine artist (ātmakṛtirvaiśilpam). The object of art, thus, represented the essential forms by following measure and proportion so that the inner beauty may become manifest.

The temple was a repository of the arts. Here was the dwelling place of the deity, just as in the body. The body of the lord is, in reality, the entire cosmos. The human body is a microcosmic parallel to the macrocosm. This was traditionally known as the parallelism of piṇḍa (body) and brahmāṇḍa (cosmos) – ‘yathā piṇḍe tathā brahmāṇḍe’. So, the whole process of tantric worship consists in exploring the body as the miniature cosmos. Various helps in this process were devised, such as the yantras or diagrams. The temple was also a three-dimensional model which exhibited the cosmic body of god (vāstupuruṣa). To understand the temple, its form and mystery, it is therefore necessary to understand the philosophy on which it rests, that is, not simply the architectonics of the Śilpaśāstras but also the philosophy of the Āgamaśāstras.

The Indus civilization already reveals the worship of Śiva in the form of the liṅga. The Vedas depict Rudra as the father and chief god of the middle region (antarikṣa). In the Nirukta of Yāska, the word ‘Rudra’ has been derived from ruda meaning ‘to howl’ and Rudra thus is the ‘howler’ referring to the storm-wind and the crashing lighting. At the same time, Rudra has also been connected with rudhra meaning dark-red. The Rudrapraśna of the Yajurveda Saṃhitā refers to Rudra as both the dark god and dark-red god. In the later Vedic Age, alongside the apotropaic aspect, Rudra was also recognized as the auspicious god or Śiva. The Yajurveda Saṃhitā, in the famous hymn to Rudra, shows on the one hand how Rudra is propitiated so that his wrath may be avoided, on the other, he showers all auspicious boons. This aspect of Rudra-Śiva is carried to its climax in the famous Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣada where his philosophical aspect is fully delineated. He is not merely the ‘Creator’, he is beyond the distinction of ‘Being and Non-Being’. He pervades all beings. He is in the child, the woman, the old man. He is

100

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 100 src=20260817_163015.jpg -->

the teacher to whom loving devotion is due. The word 'bhakti' occurs here for the first time.

In the epics, especially the Mahābhārata, the cult of Paśupati is one of the five major cults. Paśupati combines in himself the god who not only creates the world, but transcends it, and yet who is the teacher of all spiritual aspirants. Just as Bhāgvatism attracted foreign rulers, so also the Śaiva cult. Wima Kadphises is known to be a devotee of Māheśvara. In the Gupta period, Śaivism is one of the major religious cults. The Purāṇas which are generally held to have been compiled in the Gupta age contain Śaiva mythology and theology in many versions. Of the Śaiva saints of the South, the famous Nāyanāras and their hymns provide early testimony of Śaiva doctrines in the South.

Between the 8th and 12th centuries, several distinct schools of Śaiva worship and philosophy came to be formulated. The commentators on Śaṅkarācārya mention four distinct Śaiva schools- Pāśupata, Māheśvara, Śaiva Siddhāntis and Kālāmukhas. Of these, it is the Śaiva Siddhāntis who dominated Central India and the South, i.e. from Malwa to the south. In the 11th century CE, Bhoja Paramāra wrote his Tattvaprakāśa expounding the tenets of Śaiva Siddhānta. In the 14th century CE, Mādhavācārya in his Sarvadarśanasamgraha gives a clear account of the doctrines of the first three of these schools and these are the ones that are discussed here.

The general feature of Śaiva philosophy is an uncompromising theism. However, the different Śaiva schools are divided in their description of the relationship between god, man and the world. The Pāśupatas, who were perhaps the most ancient Śaiva cult, are described in the Vedānta Sūtras as clearly dualistic. God is the lord or pati, man is the paśu who is bound by pāśa or fetters. When man is freed from these bonds by the grace of god, he attains a state which is god-like but he does not become god or Śiva himself. The Māheśvaras, of whom the Kashmir Śaivas are the best known example, were divided into a dualistic as well as a non-dualistic branch. Of the non-dualistic tradition, the Malinīvijayatantra was an important example and formed the basis of the Pratyabhijñā School. This school was not only theistic but wholly monistic. It regards the world not as a delusion but as a semblance or ābhāsa of the supreme universal consciousness. It defines three different methods of spiritual practice-namely āṇavopāya, śāktopāya and śāmbhavopāya which concentrate on ritual, action and knowledge respectively. The scheme of the thirty-six tattvas is fully elaborated here.

## KASHMIR ŚAIVISM

The founder of Kashmir Śaivism is believed to have been Vasugupta, who belonged to the 8th century and is supposed to have been directed in a dream to discover the Śiva Sūtras engraved on a rock. A number of philosophers between the 8th and the 11th centuries developed this system of thought which reached its culmination in the writings of Abhinavagupta. On account of its different aspects, this system has been called the system of Trika, Pratyabhijñā or Spanda. It is a philosophy at that is both monistic and theistic.

There is a real appearance of duality, which is not an illusion but only a limited vision of the non-dual reality. Here, creation is real and yet the reality of God remains non-dual. The soul is the same as god but regards itself as different because of self-imposed limitations. Thus, in this theory, the non-duality of god is combined with the reality of creation. There is no illusionism.

The system believes in thirty-six tattvas of which twenty-five are the same as in Sāṃkhya. Beyond them, eleven more tattvas are added. These are Śiva, Śakti, Sadāśiva,

THE METAPHYSICS OF ŚAIVISM AND ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA

101

<!-- page 101 src=20260817_163025.jpg -->

Īśvara, Sadvidyā* and the six *kañcukas*. These *kañcukas* are the basic forms of bondage imposed by *māyā*. They may be regarded as the six modes of *māyā*. These are *niyati*, *kāla*, *vidyā*, *kalā*, *rāga*, *dveṣa*. The first five *tattvas* constitute the pure order, i.e. the *śuddha* *addhvā*. The rest constitute the impure order. God freely limits himself. This is called *āṇava mala*. This is the beginning of creation. Then *māyā* intervenes and imposes further *mala* or limitation. Acting under these limitations, the soul accumulates *karma* which is a third kind of limitation and is thus confined to *saṁsāra*.

Creation in this theory is the expression of the innate freedom of the supreme consciousness (*saṁvit swāntantrya*). Consciousness, thus, has an innate creative power. It has to be remembered that in reality there is only one consciousness. It follows that the human consciousness has creative power insofar as it is not tied down by the *malas*. When it is tied down by the *malas*, it enjoys a limited freedom of action called *kalā*. The nature of the created object is that of an appearance or *ābhāsa* or semblance. Time, space, individuality, action and reactions are all within this world of semblance.

The way out of the bondage produced by the ignorance of one’s own real nature is provided by self-reflection and recognition, that is, *parāmarśa* and *pratyabhijñā*. Consciousness consists of two aspects - *prakāśa* and *vimarśa*, which stand for its receptive and active aspects. Consciousness apperceives that object as if it were given from outside. In *vimarśa*, it constitutes the object by its own spontaneity. Now, given the world as objective semblances or *ābhāsa*, the way out for the individual consciousness lies in reflection over its own nature or *parāmarśa* and the recognition (*pratyabhijñā*) of his own identity with the supreme self.

The doctrine of non-dualistic Śaivism is not illusionistic like that of Śaṅkara’s Vedānta. Original Vedānta itself as a system attested by Bādarāyaṇa was also not illusionistic. It believed in Brahman as the sole efficient and material cause of the universe. This doctrine had to face the logical criticism of Buddhist philosophy which argued that creation means change which is inconsistent with eternity. How is it possible to talk of eternal consciousness in an insentient and transient world? Such a change can only be a dream-like illusion. This is the Māyāvāda or Vivartavāda which Gaupāḍa formulated and Śaṅkara accepted. This illusionistic monism, however, is not consistent with theism. Against this, Abhinavagupta has argued that ‘will’ is an essential aspect of consciousness. To be conscious and to affirm it are both integral moments of consciousness called *prakāśa* and *vimarśa*. The *svātantrya* of *saṁvit* is the identity of *prakāśa* and *vimarśa*. It follows that absolute freedom of divine consciousness is not a passive consciousness, but an infinite will which creates the world. Creation, however, is neither real transformation nor out of insentient matter nor out of nothing. It is the projection of a semblance of the self in space and time for the self under a freely assumed self limitation. This is the doctrine of Ābhāsavāda. The world is not unreal, it is the semblance of reality. The empirical self is the same as the divine spirit under self limitation.

## PĀŚUPATAS

The earliest reference to the Pāśupatas is in the *Mahābhārata* where it is referred to as one of the five religious schools. The founder of yore is said to be Śrīkaṇṭha, the son of Brahma. This tradition stating Śrīkaṇṭha to be the founder of this cult is reiterated in other texts. This very same person, sometimes also referred to as Lakuliśa or Lakuṭapāṇi, is referred to in several texts including the *Purāṇas* and inscriptions and tradition has it that he

* The *śuddha* *addhvā* has been kept in non-italics.

102

THE UDAYESVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 102 src=20260817_163029.jpg -->

belonged to a Brahmin family of Gujarat. He seems to be a historical figure. The *Pañcārthabhāṣya*, commentary on the *Pāśupatasūtras*, says that he went on foot to Ujjain and delivered his first sermon there.

The sources of Pāśupata philosophy are *Pāśupata Sūtras*, *Pañcārthabhāṣya* of Kaundinya, the *Vāyavīyasaṃhitā* of the *Śiva Purāṇa*, *Gaṇakārikā* attributed to Haradatta and its commentary *Ratnaṭīkā* of Bhāsarvajña, and the *Sarvadarśanasaṃgraha* of Mādhavācārya.

The Pāśupatas desired liberation to give them a condition like that of god. This is possible by a knowledge of the five objects or *pañcārtha*. These are *kāraṇa* (cause), *kārya* (effect), *yoga* (spiritual praxis), *vidhi* (religious rituals) and *dukhānta* (cessation of sorrow). *Pati* or god is the cause or *kāraṇa* of the creation, destruction and grace of the whole world. God is one but has infinite power of knowledge and action. He is omniscient and omnipotent. He creates the world freely and is the cause of bondage and liberation. Metaphysically, god is a unitive existence but has many aspects in which the intellect may know him. *Paśu* (invidual soul) and *pāśa* (fetters) are part of the created world, viz; *kārya*. *Vidyā* and *kalā* are both included in *pāśa*. *Vidyā* is limited power of knowledge and *kalā* is limited power of action. *Kārya*, thus, is the created world, viz. *paśu*, *pāśa*, their means and objects and all that relates to them.

*Yoga* is the joining of the soul to god through the operation of the mind. It is of two kinds, involving activity and the other, cessation of activity. The former involves *japa* (repetitive muttering of the name of the deity), *dhyāna* (meditation) etc. The cessation of *kriyā* or activity involves *niṣṭhā* or deep faith or devotion unto the lord, *saṃvit* or consciousness, *gati* or surrender to god.

*Vidhi* is the behaviour appropriate to virtuous ends. However, these included what seemed to be like antinomian practices, not to harm society but to keep away from it. Thus, the Pāśupatas hold that Lakuliśa himself prescribed bathing (or rather smearing oneself) with ashes thrice a day and sleeping on ashes. This is referred to in the Pāśupata literature and other texts as also inscriptions. *Upahāra* meant regulations. These practices were of six kinds viz, laughing, singing, dancing, the sound of *huḍḍukāra*, *namaskāra* and *japa*. Laughter meant a loud, bellowing laughter. It is interesting that *gita* refers to the compositions of Māheśvara which are in accordance with the Gāndharva system of music. The Gāndharva musical system of the *Nāṭyaśāstra* is supposed to give *adrṣṭa phala* or transcendental merit to the performer. Again, dance is mentioned as movement of the limbs according to the injunctions of the *Nāṭyaśāstra*. The sound of *huḍḍu* is the sound of the bull. It was a conventional expletive as the Vedic *vaṣaṭ*. Some of these practices were not flaunted openly. Other practices included enacting the physical expressions of a lover (*śṛṅgāraṇa*) even though not in love, showing oneself to be sleeping though not asleep, contradictory and nonsensical speech, taking the food thrown as begging etc. Actually, these practices were intended to invite ridicule for the practitioner. The public would deem such people to be unrefined and as fools. Those practitioners who were deterred by this public opprobrium would leave these practices and go back to the ways of society but those who persevered would become *yogins*. The profound truth was that the devotee underwent such practices so that (when ridiculed by society) he could be freed from the sense of false self-esteem in the public eye and inculcate a sense of complete egolessness. That, he was actually coarse or unrefined is far from the truth if he could perform the highly complicated and sophisticated Gāndharva singing or dancing according to the *Nāṭyaśāstra*.

THE METAPHYSICS OF SAIVISM AND SAIVA SIDDHANTA

103

<!-- page 103 src=20260817_163038.jpg -->

*Dukhānta* or the cessation of sorrow may be in two ways, *viz*, *anātmaka* or *sātmaka*. The former is the end of sorrow in a state which has no consciousness, such as the *mukti* of the Vaiśeṣikas, *kaivalya* of Sāṃkhya or the *nirvāṇa* of the Buddhists. *Sātmaka* is the cessation of sorrow with consciousness with knowledge and action (*dr̥kakriyāśakti*) like the Śaivas and Jainas. *Dr̥kaśakti*, this power of consciousness includes: *siddhijñāna* obtained through *darśana*, *śravaṇa*, *manana*, *vijñāna* and *sarvajñatva*. *Darśana* (seeing) means all kinds of visual knowledge which includes clairvoyance. *Śravaṇa* (hearing) includes all knowledge through hearing including the sounds beyond human range. *Manaṇa* (thinking) is the perfection of knowledge of thought in every direction. *Vijñāna* (rational knowledge) indicates here the knowledge of all sciences. *Sarvajñatva* (omniscience) is the knowledge of all possible objects. It is the power of intelligence. *Kriyāśakti* or the power of action in such a state is of three types *viz*; moving with the speed of mind (*manojavitvam*), assuming any desired form (*kāmarūpitvam*) and the power to function through any kind of organism or being (*vikaraṇadharma*). Thus, the liberated soul has the attributes of the *siddhas*, of infinite knowledge and infinite power. The soul on being so liberated does not merge with the lord, but is in complete communion with god (*śivasāyujya*). It reaches a state of god like power and knowledge, and although it is freed from suffering and bondage, it does not rest simply in infinite bliss. Because of its *aiśvarya* or power, it presumably acts as one of the perfected agencies of godhead, like a mighty angel. The hallmark of this system is that it is theistic dualism. This theism distinguishes it from Sāṃkhya and its dualism distinguishes it from Vedānta.

## ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA

The Udayeśvara temple is based on a Śaiva sect which derives from Śaiva Siddhānta. This temple was built by Udayāditya Paramāra. The Paramāra rulers were followers of this Śaiva sect. Bhoja Paramāra, one of the greatest rulers of this dynasty was a well known Śaiva philosopher himself. His *Tattvaprakāśa* is one of the most authentic texts of Śaiva Siddhānta. The text gives due attention to the practical application of philosophy to the transformation of life and the purification of the soul. It explains the bondage and liberation of the soul and the processes and stages involved in it. From being a common mortal, man may aspire to the company of the lord (*śivasāyujya*). This is the primary aim of life to be pursued in the midst of its myriad purposes. The temple is a standing reminder of this possibility.

The *Tattvaprakāśa* claims to be based on the principles of *Śaivāgamas*. The two major commentaries of the *Tattvaprakāśa* are *Tātparyadīpikā* of Śrikumāradeva and *Vṛtti* of Aghoraśivācārya. The *Tātparyadīpikā*, commenting on verse 5 of the *Tattvaprakāśa* gives the name of the following *Śaivāgamas*: *Kāmika*, *Yogaja*, *Cintya*, *Kāraṇa*, *Ajita*, *Dīpta*, *Śūkṣma*, *Sahasra*, *Amśumat*, *Suprabheda*, *Vijaya*, *Nihśvāsa*, *Svayambhuva*, *Vīra*, *Raurava*, *Makuṭa*, *Vimala*, *Candrajñāna*, *Bimba*, *Prodgīta*, *Lalita*, *Siddha*, *Santāna*, *Sarvokta*, *Parameśvara*, *Kiraṇa* and *Vātula*. The first ten seem to be the ten dualistic *Śaivāgamas* referred to in the *Kiraṇāgama*. The following seventeen are the *bhedābheda* (dualistic cum non-dualistic) *Rudrāgamas*. There are sixty four *Śaivāgamas* which are non-dualistic in nature. These are also known as *Bhairavāgamas*. There are different lists of these *abheda* or non-dualistic *Āgamas*.

The sources of Śaiva Siddhānta are said to be based upon the *Āgamas*. On the *Āgamas* are based certain standard expositions which themselves became authoritative later on. Just as for the Southern school of Śaiva Siddhāntins, the *Śivajñānabodham* of Meyakaṇdeva became the authoritative source, the *Tattvaprakāśa* of Bhoja was the most important text

104

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 104 src=20260817_163042.jpg -->

for the Northern school of Śaiva Siddhāntins. Furthermore, the two commentaries of *Tattvaprakāśa* give to this text a virtually canonical status. The *Sarvadarśanasaṅgraha* of *Mādhavācārya* gives a standard summary of the principles of these schools, relying basically on the *Mrgendrāgama* (which is basically a part of the *Kāmikāgama*) and *Tattvaprakāśa*.

The three basic constituents of Śaiva Siddhānta are *pati*, *paśu* and *pāśa*. The Śaiva Siddhāntis make an ontological difference between *pati* (god), *paśu* (individual soul) and *pāśa* (insentient elements that act as fetters which bind the soul). *Pati* is pure consciousness endowed with power and bliss. He is one without a second, the creator and lord of all. *Paśus* are *jīvas* or individual souls. Even though there are a plurality of souls in the Śaiva texts, they are generally referred to in the singular number as *paśu* in the universal sense. *Paśus* are also conscious beings from all eternity, individuals and infinite in number but they are not free, though they are capable of freedom with the grace of the lord (*pati*). The *paśu* is covered with impurities (*mala*), different kinds of *mala* associated with it from beginningless time and thus go through the cycle of birth and rebirth. Though the souls are the same, yet some are in a state of bondage and some in a state of liberation. Those in bondage, too, may be in different conditions of progress. The differences of souls are due to the conditioning factor of *mala* and *karma*. These souls are in different states of knowledge because of the fact that their knowledge is obscured by the various coverings of *mala*. But even though the souls or *paśus* are associated with *mala* or impurities, they are pervaded by Śiva, and gradually as the impurities are purged, the *jīva* becomes more and more pure, until it gets liberation and becomes like Śiva. The Śaiva Siddhānta system postulates thirty-six *tattvas* or basic constituents of reality. Out of these twenty-five are broadly similar to the division found in Sāṃkhya, though with some differences, especially in their ontological and cosmological status. To these are added eleven more; six being the subtle causes of the created world and five being transcendent and eternal. These five are called the *śuddha addhvā* (pure path). All the *tattvas* here are timeless aspects of the same eternal reality which is Śiva. It is called pure path because the soul in its process of emancipation moves up from the impure path of creation to the pure path of eternity. Śiva is the primal source of all the *tattvas*.

The first five pure principles are Śiva, Śakti, Sadāśiva, Īśvara and Vidyā. They are different moments of infinite consciousness having infinite knowledge and will, *jñānaśakti* and *kriyāśakti*. This is eternal, though we can intellectually distinguish different moments in it. Śiva is the perennial source of all the *tattvas*. He is the nature of infinite knowledge, power and bliss. He is one without a second, the sole cause of the universe, the lord of the destiny of the souls and the supreme savior. Śiva and Śakti are distinct but not entitatively or qualitatively different. Śiva and Śakti are essentially one but conceptually distinguished as 'Being' and 'Energy'. Śakti is the power or the energy of Śiva, conceived as functioning by his nature. Sadāśiva is the lord with a dominant inward consciousness in which the universe to be created is reflected potentially, as in a mirror. Sadāśiva is also the source of the five *āmanāyas*, and thus the eternal source of grace as the eternal teacher. Īśvara is the lord as creator of the universe and the controller of the destiny of the *jīva*. Sadvidyā is the nature of objectified consciousness which contains the seed of all things. Thus, in summing up, one may say that in *śuddha addhvā* there is only eternal reality of the nature of Śiva but it has five moments or aspects in relation to creation. Śiva, in a latent mode, is a purely transcendent aspect. Śiva in the mode of creativity is Śakti. Śiva is distinguished from Śakti when there is the intention to create. Śakti is the first shining forth (*unmeṣa*). Sadāśiva is the moment when the intention to create becomes a balance

THE METAPHYSICS OF ŚAIVISM AND ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA

105

<!-- page 105 src=20260817_163050.jpg -->

between knowledge and action. Sadāśiva is also visualized as the eternal source of spirituality or grace and has been pictured as having five faces. When the power of action becomes dominant, Śiva is called Īśvara. When the power of action becomes subordinated to the power of knowledge then it is Vidyā. This is predominantly of the nature of knowledge.

The impure path consists of māyā and its five kañcukas or veils. Māyā is the primary substance of the created world. It is on the one hand, the limiting power of the lord called tirodhāna śakti, on the other, the primary substance of the world (vastu). Māyā in this system is not an illusion, but something real and objective but it is not independent of Śiva. From māyā proceed kāla, niyati, kalā, vidyā and rāga which are the bondages in which the soul functions. Being thus subject to ignorance and desire, the soul engages in karma.

The soul is of a nature similar to Śiva and in this respect is quite different from the puruṣa of Sāṃkhya which has no power of any kind. The jīva, subject to pāśa is called paśu. The jīva is covered by a beginningless mala or impurity which robs it of its natural power. Māyā gives to the jīva limited powers as if partly unveiling its nature, but it is also one of the pāśa which binds the souls to the world. Of its five forms, vidyā is finite, object knowledge, compounded of both knowledge and ignorance. The knowledge of the soul distinguishes the different objects without knowing their ultimate unity as grounded in māyā or dependence on Śiva. In so far that the jīva is ignorant of his own nature or Śiva, his knowledge is only a part of avidyā. Kalā is limited will or conative power. Rāga is desire and attachment. With these three, the soul becomes a conscious agent pursuing and willing objects of desire. This process takes place in time (kalā) and ordered space. These are kāla and niyati. These may be described as the metapsychic principles of the system, that is to say, they are metaphysical principles underlying its psychology. The concepts of māyā and paśu are quite different from Sāṃkhya. At the same time their dualism and realism differentiate them from Advaita. For the fructification of karma, the soul needs a suitable environment. For this, the further process of creation takes place. It begins with avyakta down to mahābhūta. These twenty-four tattva are similar to Sāṃkhya. The avyakta is the unformed, primary state of the created world just as clay is of the pot. It consists of the three guṇa and ultimately of sukha, dukha and moha. Out of it proceeds the principle of Intelligence (buddhi). The description of buddhi in the Tattvaprakāśa appears to take buddhi as the individual psyche. But in Sāṃkhya it has a clearly cosmic aspect. From buddhi comes the principle of egoity or ahaṃkāra. This ahaṃkāra has three aspects; subjectivity, self-consciousness and the biotic force (jīvana śakti). From ahaṃkāra proceed sixteen elements, five jñānendriya, five karmendriya, manas and five tanmātra. The five jñānendriya are the sense organs of knowledge, viz. hearing, touching, seeing, tasting and smelling. The five karmendriya are tongue, hands, feet, pāyu (anus) and upastha (generative organs). Manas is the power of synthesizing the sensations into percepts. It has also the power of willing, desiring, remembering, hoping, feeling, etc. The five tanmātra are the five sense data, viz. sound, touch, colour, taste and smell. From the tanmātra proceed pañcamahābhūta or the five gross elements of ether, air, fire, water and earth.

The created world has, thus, one unmanifest (avyakta) and twenty-three manifest constituents. The twenty-fifth constituent is puruṣa in Sāṃkhya but is replaced by jīva or paśu here, both of which have a different nature.

The nature of paśu is essentially similar to that of Śiva. The difference is created by the factor of mala. However, it should not be construed to be like that of paramāṇu which

<!-- page 106 src=20260817_163057.jpg -->

is essentially insentient and finite. But *paśu* is essentially all pervasive and sentient. The *paśu* is bound by fetters (*pāśa*) of a beginningless *mala* (impurity), by his own actions in the *jīva*. *Karma* is the flow of action (both meritorious or otherwise) and consequences which bind the individual within the world of his own desires. The commentators of the *Tattvaprakāśa*, Aghoraśivācārya and Śrīkumāradeva give their comments on this. Aghoraśivācārya states that *māyā* means the contingent bonds of passion etc., which are a result of the *karmas*. *Māyā* has a dual function, i.e. it covers the truth and also that is reveals what is not true. Śrīkumāradeva quotes from the *Kiraṇāgama* saying that the impurity (*malinātva*) is present in the *aṇu* (*paśu*) from beginningless time. The purity in his a cause, the occasion for this should be stated. This produces a character that is dual, pure and impure at the same time. Pure crystal and patinated copper (are the two examples of duality); just as in this case (of crystal and copper) there is no occasion (for their dual appearance).

The question that arises then is what is the cause of this *mala*? Where does it come from? It comes from the *rodha śakti* of Śiva. *Rodha śakti* is the power of god which operates from its base in the *pāśas*. Mādhavācārya in the seventh chapter of *Sarvadarśanasāṅgraha* quotes from the *Mrgendrāgama* and says that “the power of Śiva is the only power. It is the source of all powers. It seems to function as the *pāśa* only as a matter of appearance.” But how can god be the cause of bondage? “How can the same Śakti or power belong at once to *pati* and *paśu*? True, power really belongs to the lord and it is only in a subordinate and metaphorical way that it is said to belong to *pāśa*.” In other words, the only independent source of power is Śiva. The power of *pāśa* is derived from the power of Śiva and illustrates that function of Śiva that is called *nigraha* or *tirodhāna* without which creation is not possible. The purpose of creation is to provide the souls with an environment in which they can experience the consequences of their *karma*. *Karma* would not be possible unless the souls were covered with *mala* and bound by *pāśa* arising from *māyā*. If there were no covering or limitation on the soul, it would not engage in *karma* and hence the world would not be needed.

Śrīkumāradeva says that the five objects of Śiva’s power which are of the nature of *pāśa* are *mala*, *karma*, *sūkṣma-māyā*, *sthūla māyā* and *pāśānugrāhaka śakti*. *Mala* is *mala*, *karma* is *karma*, *sūkṣma-māyā* is *māyā*, *sthūla māyā* is *māyottha* (or that arising from *māyā* or *jagat*), *pāśanugrāhaka śakti* is *tirodhāna śakti*. Śrīkumāra further quotes from the *Śaivarahasya* and *Prayogamañjarī* which counter this by stating that there are only three types of bondages, viz. *mala*, *karma* and *māyā*. The commentator, however, says that this is not so, as *māyā* is of two kinds, viz. *sūkṣma* (causal) and *sthūla* (manifest) and all things rest on *tirodhāna śakti*. The *jīva* or the *paśu* bound by the five objects or *arthapañcaka* goes through the cycles of birth and rebirth and wanders in *saṁsāra*. Gradually, as he works for liberation and through the grace of Śiva, the *paśu* carves a way out of *saṁsāra* or *aśuddha addhvā*. From being *sakala*, he becomes *pralayākala* and then *vijñānākala* and then through the special grace of Śiva, he moves to the *śuddha addhvā*.

The four *pādas* are the four elements which help the liberation of the *paśu*. *Vidyā* comes first as it shows the true nature and relationship of the *paśu* to the *pati* and the *pāśas*. *Kriyā* begins with *dīkṣā* or initiation. *Yoga* is chiefly the practice of the internal discipline of the mind. *Caryā* is the regulation of the external conduct.

*Paśus* are of three types, viz. *vijñānākala*, *pralayākala* and *sakala*. *Vijñānākala* is free of the bondages of *māyā* and *karma*. These are of two types, viz. those *vijñānakevalī* who are

THE METAPHYSICS OF SAIVISM AND SAIVA SIDDHANTA

107

<!-- page 107 src=20260817_163109.jpg -->

free from *mala* (*samāptakaluṣa*) and those who are not (*asamāptakaluṣa*). The former attain that state through knowledge or *yoga*, *saṃnyāsa* (renunciation), through *bhoga* (through experience of pleasure and pain one gradually obtains detachment), or through *karmakṣaya* (cessation of action). These are uplifted into the *vidyātattva* as *vidyeśvaras*. They, thus, attain *śivasāyuja* or the company of Śiva. The names of the eight *vidyeśvaras* are given as Ananta, Sūkṣma, Śivottama, Ekanetra, Ekarudra, Trimutri, Śrīkaṇṭha and Śikhandī. The names of these eight *vidyeśvaras* are given in the *Āgamas* (for eg. *Kāmikāgama*) which imbue their divine forms with iconographical descriptions. But what of those *vijñānākevalis* who are not rid of their *mala*? They cannot attain to *śivasāyuja* because they still have *mala*, but they cannot return to the world because they have exhausted their *karma*. They cannot reside in any of the three *lokas*. Śrīkumāradeva says that these have to work out their *mala* as they are given *dīkṣā* and with this indirect knowledge (*parokṣajñāna*) they undergo a period of spiritual training. Direct knowledge (*aparokṣajñāna*) cannot arise within them unless obstruction caused by *mala* is destroyed. Thus undergoing this spiritual praxis, at the end of which when the *mala* matures, they are fit for the grace of Śiva and obtain liberation as *mantreśvaras* or *mantradevatās*. The *Tattvaprakāśa* gives their number as seven crores. The *Vṛtti* says they are given the forms of pure *bindus* or ethereal bodies. The category of *pralayākala* is the one in which both *mala* and *karma* remain. But when *mala* and *karma* ripen and mature, then there are various ways of liberation. A few and limited number become *mantreśvaras*. Some are appointed by the lord as *bhuvampatis*. Some re-enter the world of *sakalas* in subtle bodies known as *puryastake*; the five sense organs, *manas*, *buddhi* and *ahaṃkāra* are its eight constituents. The *sakalas* are associated with *mala*, *māyā* and *karma*. They wander from one birth to another undergoing the manifold experiences of the world or *saṃsāra*, till their impurities get ripened. It is then that they receive the grace of god through proper initiation by which their impurities are removed and they may obtain liberation. It is interesting that Śiva is referred to as the Ācāryamūrti or teacher for initiation of those souls whose impurities have ripened.

How this graded classification of the *paśus* has been reached is not wholly certain. On the one hand, it seems to be based on the logical elimination of the three sources of bondage, one by one, from the gross to the subtle. But that each of these is totally eliminated is, however, a supposition which is logically questionable because what it means is that the effect is eradicated first and the cause afterwards. Perhaps there is an attenuation of the force of the grosser bonds. However, the basis of the doctrine is Āgamic. We could compare this doctrine with that of some other schools which believe in gradual progress towards emancipation. Thus, the Sāṃkhya *puruṣa* is free from contact with *prakṛti*, i.e. he is *akala* and also free from *mala* and *karma* but he is pure consciousness without the power of action. This distinguishes him from the liberated souls of Śaiva Siddhānta which have the power of action. What separates the *jīva* from Śiva is the three-fold of *mala*, *karma* and *māyā*. The grace of Śiva gradually eliminates these and the *jīva* is restored to its original pure status. Like other Śaiva systems, Śaiva Siddhānta, too, accepts the five-fold activities of Śiva, viz. *sṛṣṭi* (creation), *sthiti* (maintenance), *saṃhāra* (destruction), *tirobhāva* (non-revealing or veiling which is the source of obscuration and thus of bondage) and *anugraha* (grace). Mādhavācārya, in his *Sarvadarsanaśaṃgraha* quotes this verse of Bhoja. He, however, makes an interesting observation. He says that these are the perennial functions which are performed directly by Śiva in *śuddha addhvā*, but in the *aśuddha addhvā*, they are performed by Ananta etc and he quotes the *Kiraṇāgama* to that effect. The *Tattvaprakāśa* also refers to the body of Śiva as consisting of the five *mantras*. Mādhavācārya elucidates this in his *Sarvadarśanasaṃgraha*. He says that god does not

108

THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 108 src=20260817_163114.jpg -->

have a natural or prākṛt body which is born of mala, karma and pāśa. One speaks metaphorically of his body in terms of the mantras. The five mantras, Īśāna etc., are of the nature of Śakti and constitute his body. Īśāna is the head, Tatpurusa is the face, Aghora is the heart, Vāmadeva constitutes the private parts and Sadyojāta the feet. These five (Tatpuruṣa), adanalakṣaṇa or saṃhāra or destruction (Aghora), sthitilakṣaṇa or maintenance (Vāmadeva) and udbhavalakṣaṇa or creation (Sadyojāta). This body is not like our own body but created of his own will. Mādhavācārya then quotes the Mṛgendrāgama saying that, not being born of malas etc., this is the Śākta body. Also, that his body is composed of the five mantras, Īśāna etc. The Sarvadarśanasamgraha says that, although the lord has no form, nonetheless, these forms are assumed by the lord for the sake of helping the devotee to contemplate upon him. The Sarvadarśanasamgraha quotes the Pauṣkarāgama saying, “This form is assumed for the help of the devotee.” Other Āgamas are also mentioned by saying that “the lord is worshipped regularly as possessing a form, because the mind cannot contemplate on anything which is devoid of form.” The commentaries give two different expositions of the relationship of Śiva and māyā. According to Śrīkumāradeva, Śiva is both the instrumental and material cause of the universe. He, however, operates through māyā which is dependent on Śiva, but has a different and insentient existence. Māyā is insentient and changeable and provides the material substance for every created thing. It has two forms, viz. an original form called bindu, in which ignorance predominates and which envelops the paśu or aṇu and constitutes its basic mala. From this, karma arises. In the third place, māyā and its powers called māyīya constitute the third obscuration of the soul. Thus the three, mala, karma and māyā, along with māyīya constitute the pāśas. According to Śrīkumāradeva, bindu and māyā are the external powers of Śiva-bāhya śakti.

Aghoraśivācārya emphasized the distinction of “I” from “Śiva” and its role as the material cause of the universe. In this entire tradition, dualistic Śaiva philosophy is characterized by the fact that Śiva is only the instrumental cause of the world.

In the Tattvaprakāśa, māyā is conceived, on the one hand, as the everlasting material out of which the world has been created. Here, māyā is like the prakṛti of Sāṃkhya. It is one and all, subtle and invisible, but is capable of being transformed into all created objects. Māyā as the primary material of creation is common to all jīvas. The nature of this common universal material of creation is its capacity of producing inconscience (mohasañjananī). From māyā, Śiva creates the body, senses and the environmental world (tanu, karaṇa, bhuvaṇa) and gives them to each soul in accordance with his action.

In Sāṃkhya, prakṛti acts by itself, unconsciously but autonomously for the sake of the souls just as the cow gives milk spontaneously for the calf, ‘vatsavivṛddhinimittam’ (Sāṃkhyakārikā). But in Śaiva Siddhānta, māyā is acted upon by Śiva through his powers. Māyā is the material acted upon by Śiva. Śiva is eternal because he is timeless. But māyā is not eternal in this sense. Māyā is an everlasting process in time. Māyā is changeable, where as Śiva is unchangeable. Śiva is independent, but māyā is dependent on him.

The other aspect of māyā is that it is the power which binds the soul. It is one of the pāśas with its five forms of kāla, niyati, vidyā, rāga and kalā. Thus, one aspect of māyā is that it is like the prakṛti of Sāṃkhya and the other, that it is a binding force of the jīvas, i.e. the pāśas. Now jīva or “I” in Sāṃkhya is eternally pure and devoid of all power. But through a beginningless ignorance, he identifies himself with prakṛti. In Śaiva Siddhānta, jīva is eternally capable of knowledge and power and through god’s grace, of acquiring a status similar to Śiva, though subordinate to him. In Sāṃkhya, the knowledge and power of puruṣa is a loan from prakṛti, the puruṣa only being a transcendent subjectivity.

THE METAPHYSICS OF ŚAIVISM AND ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA

109

<!-- page 109 src=20260817_163130.jpg -->

In Śaiva Siddhānta, the *jīva* has powers belonging to his own nature but covered by *mala*, *karma* and the *kañcukas* of *māyā*. Basically god gives to each soul his body, senses and empirical placement in accordance with his *karma*. In Sāṃkhya, *karma* decides what the soul is to get. No god is needed. Sāṃkhya accepts teleology but not theology. Śaiva Siddhānta is a theistic system. The twenty-three principles of Sāṃkhya are psychophysical principles. They are merely classifications of universal experiences which are generally accepted in most Hindu philosophical systems with minor differences.

*Prakṛti* is a cosmological principle. It is atheistically understood in Sāṃkhya. *Prakṛti* is the ultimate cause. In Sāṃkhya we have *pariṇāmavāda*, i.e. the earlier principles are the causes of the later principles which are other manifestations. The effect is latent in the cause. In Śaiva Siddhānta, we find *iśvarakāraṇavāda*. Unlike Sāṃkhya, Śaiva Siddhānta believes in god as the cause of all creation. Here, *prakṛti* is also acted upon by Śiva and through his activation produces other *tattvas*.

This reduction of the material to the psychic in Sāṃkhya enables it to relate the process of material evolution to the fulfillment of the purpose. In Śaiva Siddhānta, the common material cause of the world is used by Śiva to create the intended world. Śiva is the efficient cause of the world. *Prakṛti* called *māyā* is the material cause of the world. In Sāṃkhya, there is no efficient cause, causation being regarded only as self-transformation [*pariṇāma*]. The relationship of *māyā* to Śiva remains the most difficult philosophical and theological question. In the pure Advaitic system of Vedānta, *māyā* is simply a beginningless illusion which has no reality in Brahman. In the purely dualistic systems there is an insentient material cause of the world on which the will of god acts. Here too, there is no place for any real *māyā*, though a beginningless ignorance has to be accepted for the *jīva*. In the Advaitic Śaiva systems, which are not illusionistic, there is no real material cause outside Śiva. *Māyā* is one of the *malas* which explains the limitations of the *jīva*. The dualistic Śaiva Siddhānta regards *māyā* as the material cause of the universe which is subject to the will of god, but seeks to give some explanation of the fact that the creative will of Śiva and the material cause of the universe cooperate in the creative process. If *māyā* as the material cause of the world is called *bindu*, the will of god insofar as it operates on the *bindu* has to be regarded as a kind of counterpart to it, namely as a pure, super *bindu*. So, in the southern Śaiva Siddhānta this pure *bindu* has been called *mahāmāyā*.

The system is dualistic in several ways, though it has intimations of non-dualism also. In the first place, although the *paśu* has a nature essentially similar to the *pati* or Śiva and attains to it in liberation, he is never actually merges in Śiva. There is an entitative difference between *paśu* (souls) and *pati* (Śiva). Secondly, the created world is not an illusion, nor does it ever disappear because *māyā* is regarded as perennial in nature. Thirdly, Śiva himself is not a pure, undifferentiated being. He has distinctions of moment and aspect within the changelessness of his eternity.

## SOUTHERN ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA

The southern Śaiva Siddhānta derives from early sources, the most important of which were the *Śaivāgamas* and the hymns of the Śaiva saints or Nāyanārs. Of the *Āgamas*, the *Kāmika* with *Mṛgendra* as also *Rauravāgama* were the most important. Tradition speaks of sixty-three Nāyanārs, whose lives are described in the *Periya Purāṇam*. The Nāyanārs are generally held to have flourished during the Pallava period. The Pallava ruler, Rājasimha, has been referred to as ‘*Śaivasiddhānta-mārga-kṣatasakalāmalah*’ and ‘*Āgamānusarī*’. Tirumular, an early Śaiva saint, says that the *Āgama* is as much the work

110

THE UDAYESVARA TEMPLE

<!-- page 110 src=20260817_163136.jpg -->

of god as the Veda. The four great teachers of Śaivism (samayācāryas) are Appar, Tirujñānasambandhar, Sundaramūrti and Māṇikkavācakar. The hymns sung by the first three are compiled as the Tevaram, while those of Māṇikkavācakar constitute the Tiruvācakam.

By the 11th century CE and later, we have two important Śaiva writers, viz. Nāmpiyandār and Sekkilār. Śaiva-Siddhānta blossomed fully in the 13th century with the works of the great Śaiva teachers (santānācāryas). The most important was Meyakaṇḍadeva as also his pupils, Aruṇandi and Umāpati. Of the Nāyanār saints, the work of Māṇikkavācakar has received special significance through the commentary of Umāpati. Indeed, the meaning of his name is “he whose utterances are rubies”. The Tiruvācakam is essentially a devotional, poetic work which celebrates the omnipresence and sovereign power of Śiva. He is the source of grace (arul). Without his grace, the soul cannot be liberated from the three-fold bondage in which it is fettered. The vision of Śiva fills the poet with joy and wonder in the universe. He sings and dances out of this beatific joy. Although Śaiva Siddhānta has a complex system of how the souls get bound and the many stages they progress towards liberation, here the whole process is suffused in a sea of devotion in which the soul is alternately abased as a mere humble servitor of the lord and, then again, as elevated by his grace into a state of virtual union with him. Here, the Siddhānta assumes the aspect or pure devotionalism in which prescriptions and ritual seem to give way to mystic rapture. This reminds one of the distinction between vāthi and parā bhakti, the former leading to the latter.

The concept of god as love and grace is a dominant theme in the hymns of the Śaiva saints. According to Tirumalār, only the ignorant distinguish between god (Śiva) and love (anbu) and wisdom lies in identifying the two. In fact, the state of grace or arul is distinguished from the state of darkness or irul as also that of confused knowledge or marul.

Meyakaṇḍadeva wrote his Śivajñānbodha in the 13th century and this is one of the most important works of Southern Śaiva-Siddhānta philosophy. According to tradition, this work is actually Meyakaṇḍadeva’s Tamil rendering of the Pāśavimocana section of the Rauravāgama. However, some scholars opine that this Tamil text is an original work of Meyakaṇḍadeva. There are twelve sūtras (aphorisms) in the Śivajñānabodha with a vārtika (commentary) written by the author himself.

A brief comment on the philosophy of the twelve sūtras has been attempted here. In the first sūtra of Śivajñānabodha, the world is referred to as he, she and it. God is the creator, sustainer and destroyer of the world. The first sūtra establishes the existence of god as the cause of the world by the inference that the world is involved in change and, therefore, cannot be everlasting or self-supporting. This is only a brief statement of the well-known cosmological argument elaborated in all theistic philosophies like Nyāya-well-known cosmological argument elaborated in all theistic philosophies like Vaiśeṣika and Vedānta. The world is a transient effect and needs a conscious cause. The second sūtra describes the relationship of the soul to god. The relationship is neither ‘abheda, bheda or bhedābheda’. God’s jñānaśakti is related to the soul by the relation of inherence (samavāya) and it is god’s will that makes the soul a part of the process of creation. The third sūtra, in substance, summarizes the well-known Vedāntic concept of the distinction of the soul from the body and the three states of waking, dreaming and sleeping. The body is an instrument fashioned by māyā and used by the soul. The fourth sūtra elaborates the very notion of the transcendence of the soul to the body and the mind. The fifth establishes the insentient nature of the senses and also the powerlessness of the soul to know itself or god without this process being quickened by the grace (arul)

THE METAPHYSICS OF ŚAIVISM AND ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA | 111

<!-- page 111 src=20260817_163141.jpg -->

of god. The sixth *sūtra* distinguishes *sat* from *asat*. *Asat* is the transient objective existence of the sentient world. What is capable of being so perceived and yet is never so perceived by the senses but which is the ultimate principle of intelligence that is *śivasat* or *cibat*. This supersensible reality in its fullness is divine and lies beyond the power of human understanding but its reality as Being or Presence can be understood by the grace of god. In other words, by the grace of god, one may apprehend that god exists and is the source of the world but one cannot understand the true and complete nature of god. The seventh *sūtra* says that the soul lies between *sat* and *asat*, i.e. god and the world. It has, therefore, an intermediate nature and is described as *satasat*. According to *sūtra* eight, after one has performed right actions and *tapas*, he receives the grace of god through a preceptor in which the power of god himself grants him grace. The soul realizes the worthlessness of the life of the world and turns to divine worship. The ninth *sūtra* speaks of *pāśa* and *pati*. The soul realizes the world (*pāśa* or that which binds) is false like a mirage and seeks recourse in *pati* or the lord. This *sūtra* highlights the importance of contemplation of *mantra* with rituals. Finally as a result of its worship, the soul is purified and the tenth *sūtra* says that as such it is pervaded by god and released from its bondage of *malas*. According to the next *sūtra*, just as the soul animates the senses, the grace of god animates the soul. The soul is thus able to see that its own pure nature is not differing from god. The twelfth *sūtra* may be summarized by saying that the soul, liberated from *malas*, is still expected to abide in the company of spiritual souls or *bhaktas* and contemplate upon the forms of the temples as his own forms.

It might seem that there is a contradiction between the earlier *bheda*, *abheda* and *bhedābheda* and the concluding assertions. This, however, is not true because the earlier denial refers to the soul in the condition of bondage, the latter to the soul in the condition of liberation. The Śaiva tradition has consistently held that Śiva and *jīva* have the same nature except that the *jīva* may be bound by *māyā* and remains dependent on Śiva.
